In this section, we will apply our general framework of SRMM (
8
) to derive convergence results for variants of PSGD such as the ‘double-averaging PSGD’ due to Nesterov and Shikhman
[
57
]
as well as its generalization.

Suppose we have a prescribed weight sequence
(w_{n})_{m\geq 1}
and hyperparameters
L,\lambda\geq 0
. Consider the following iterates

(45)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\overline{\nabla}_{n}&\leftarrow(1-w_{n})\overline{\nabla}_{n-1}+w_{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})\qquad(\overline{\nabla}_{0}:=\mathbf{0})\\
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}&\leftarrow(1-w_{n})\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-2}+w_{n}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\qquad(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0})\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}&\leftarrow\frac{L}{L+\lambda}\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}+\frac{\lambda}{L+\lambda}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\qquad(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0})\\
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}&\leftarrow\textup{Proj}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}-\frac{1}{L+\lambda}\overline{\nabla}_{n}\right).\end{cases}

Setting
\lambda=0
reduces (
45
) to the following ‘double-averaging PSGD’, which was first investigated by Nesterov and Shikhman
[
57
]
:

(46)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\overline{\nabla}_{n}&\leftarrow(1-w_{n})\overline{\nabla}_{n-1}+w_{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})\qquad(\overline{\nabla}_{0}:=\mathbf{0})\\
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}&\leftarrow(1-w_{n})\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-2}+w_{n}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\qquad(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0})\\
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}&\leftarrow\textup{Proj}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}-\frac{1}{L}\overline{\nabla}_{n}\right).\end{cases}

Compared to the standard PSGD update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\leftarrow\textup{Proj}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}-\frac{1}{L}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k-1})\right)
with fixed step size
1/L
, the algorithm (
46
) uses the recursively averaged gradient
\overline{\nabla}_{n}
as well as the recursively averaged iterate
\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}
. The update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\leftarrow\textup{Proj}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}-\frac{1}{L}\overline{\nabla}_{n}\right)
, where one uses the averaged gradients in place of the stochastic gradient, is known as ‘dual-averaging’
[
53
,
71
]
. For convex objectives in the online setting with i.i.d. observations,
[
71
]
obtained a bound on the regret of this method of order
O(\sqrt{t})
. Compared to such dual-averaging methods, the double-averaging method (
46
) uses additional inline averaging of the iterates, which is known to be equivalent to using a momentum
[
14
]
.

In
[
57
]
, Nestrove and Shikhman showed that for convex (possibly non-smooth) objective functions in the offline setting, the double-averaging method (
46
) generates a sequence of iterates that decreases the optimality gap in the objective value as
O(t^{-1/2})
. Moreover, such a rate of convergence is justified for the
whole sequence
of test points for the first time for subgradient methods. However, to the author’s best knowledge, the double-averaging method has not been analyzed beyond this setting. This is in contrast to the standard PSGD case, which has been analyzed for general non-smooth nonconvex objectives under i.i.d.
[
13
]
and Markovian
[
1
]
data assumption. As an application of our general SRMM analysis, we will derive very general convergence and complexity results for the iterates (
45
) (and hence for its specialization (
46
) as well).

Assuming the per-sample loss function
f_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\ell(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})
is
L
-smooth for each data point
\mathbf{x}
, the following prox-linear surrogate (see Ex.
B.3
) is indeed a majorizing surrogate of
f_{n}
:

(47)

\displaystyle g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})+\langle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}.

Now we claim that the generalized double-averaging scheme (
45
) is in fact equivalent to the following SRMM iterate

(48)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}\right),

where
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=(1-w_{n})\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+w_{n}g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
denotes the recursive average of the surrogates as in (
8
). Indeed, letting
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}:=\frac{L}{L+\lambda}\bar{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}+\frac{\lambda}{L+\lambda}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
, one can easily see that (
48
) is equivalent to

(49)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\,\,\langle\overline{\nabla}_{n},\,\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle+\frac{L+\lambda}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}-(L+\lambda)\left\langle\boldsymbol{\theta},\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}\right\rangle

(50)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\,\,\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}-\frac{1}{L+\lambda}\overline{\nabla}_{n})\right\rVert^{2}

(51)

\displaystyle=\textup{Proj}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n-1}-\frac{1}{L+\lambda}\overline{\nabla}_{n}\right).

Interestingly, the above derivation also tells us that the double-averaging scheme (
46
) is equivalent to the SMM update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
with the prox-linear surrogates in (
47
). Using the additional proximal regularization with parameter
\lambda\geq 0
as in (
48
) has the effect of additional averaging of the parameters, giving a constant weight
\frac{\lambda}{L+\lambda}
to the most recent iterate
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
(see (
45
)) instead of the possibly decaying weight
w_{n}
(see (
46
)).

Now that we have verified the generalized double-averaging PSGD (
45
) is a special instance of SRMM (
8
) with prox-linear surrogate and proximal regularizer, we obtain the following general convergence and complexity results for the (
45
) as well as (
46
).

Corollary 5.1

(Convergence and complexity of double-averaging PSGD)
.

Theorems
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
hold for the double-averaging PSGD (
46
) (case
C1
with prox-linear surrogates) and its generalization (
45
) (case
C3
with prox-linear surrogates and proximal regularization).

Next, our general framework enables us to consider a block coordinate version of the double-averaging PSGD (
46
), which will be an example of Algorithm
1
in case
C2
. For instance, instead of updating the entire parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
, we may only update its
i
th block
\theta_{n}^{(i)}
where
i
is chosen uniformly at random independently at each iteration:

(52)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i&\sim\textup{Uniform}(\{1,\dots,m\})\\
\overline{\nabla}_{n}^{(i)}&\leftarrow(1-w_{n})\overline{\nabla}_{n-1}^{(i)}+w_{n}\nabla_{\theta^{(i)}}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})\qquad(\overline{\nabla}_{0}^{(i)}:=\mathbf{0})\\
\bar{\theta}_{n-1}^{(i)}&\leftarrow(1-w_{n})\bar{\theta}_{n-2}^{(i)}+w_{n}\theta_{n-1}^{(i)}\qquad(\bar{\theta}_{0}^{(i)}:=\theta_{0}^{(i)})\\
\theta_{n}^{(i)}&\leftarrow\textup{Proj}_{\Theta^{(i)}\cap\{\theta\,:\,\lVert\theta-\theta_{n-1}^{(i)}\rVert\leq w_{n}\}}\left(\bar{\theta}_{n-1}^{(i)}-\frac{1}{L}\overline{\nabla}_{n}^{(i)}\right).\end{cases}

Note that in (
52
), we used the diminishing radius regularization to update
\theta_{n}^{(i)}
. Also notice that the above block version of (
46
) has the computational advantage that one only needs to compute the partial stochastic gradient
\nabla_{\theta^{(i)}}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k-1})
instead of the full stochastic gradient
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k-1})
, which could be significant when the parameter dimension
p
is large. For instance, here we can choose individual coordinates as single blocks so the per-iteration computational cost of (
52
) is of
O(1)
plus the cost of sampling the new data point
\mathbf{x}_{n}
and for the projection onto the convex set
\Theta^{(i)}\cap\{\theta\,:\,\lVert\theta-\theta_{n-1}^{(i)}\rVert\leq w_{n}\}
.

As before, our general result immediately implies the following convergence and complexity result for the randomized block variant of the double-averaging PSGD (
52
).

Corollary 5.2

(Convergence and complexity of double-averaging PSGD)
.

Theorems
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
hold for the randomized block variant of the double-averaging PSGD (
52
) (case
C2
with prox-linear surrogates and randomized coordinate descent)

We remark that the above corollary holds if we used cyclic block coordinate descent in (
52
) instead of randomly choosing block coordinates to update.

5.1.2.
Proximal point empirical loss minimization

Consider the following iterates (
53
), where we recursively update the empirical loss function
\bar{f}_{n}
and minimize is proximal point modification
\tilde{f}_{n}
over the parameter space
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

(53)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})&\leftarrow(1-w_{n})\bar{f}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+w_{n}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta})\\
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}&\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\,\left(\tilde{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}\right)\end{cases}

Under
(A1)
, each
\bar{f}_{n}
is
L
-smooth so if
\lambda>L
, then the proximal point modification
\tilde{f}_{n}
is
(\lambda-L)
-strongly convex (see Lemma
C.3
). The proximal point method has been used in empirical loss minimization problems with convex objectives
[
20
,
40
]
.

It is easy to see that (
53
) is an instance of case
C3
, where we use the trivial surrogate
g_{n}(\cdot)=\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\cdot)
. Hence our general results yield that the proximal point ERM (
53
) in the general non-convex constrained setting with dependent data.

Corollary 5.3

(Convergence and complexity of Proximal Point ERM)
.

Theorems
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
hold for the proximal point ERM (
53
).

5.1.3.
Online (Nonnegative) Matrix Factorization

Consider the matrix factorization loss
f(W,H):=\lVert X-WH\rVert^{2}+\lambda\lVert H\rVert_{1}
, where
X\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
is a given data matrix to be factorized into the product of dictionary
W\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}
and the code
H\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
with
\lambda\geq 0
being the
L_{1}
-regularization parameter for
H
. Clearly
f
is two-block multi-convex and differentiable with respect to
W
with gradient
\nabla_{W}f(W,H)=(X-WH)H^{T}
. Fix compact and convex constraint sets
\Theta_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}
and
\Theta_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
. Then
W\mapsto\nabla f(W,H)
and
H\mapsto\nabla f(W,H)
are both Lipschitz over the compact and convex set
\Theta_{1}\times\Theta_{2}
.

Suppose we have a sequence of data matrices
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and a sequence of weights
(w_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. For each
n\geq 1
, the function
f_{n}:W\mapsto\ell(X_{n},W)=\inf_{H\in\Theta_{2}}\lVert X_{n}-WH\rVert^{2}+\lambda\lVert H\rVert_{1}
is the minimum reconstruction error for factorizing
X_{n}
using the dictionary matrix
W
. The corresponding empirical loss minimization problem is

(54)

\displaystyle W_{n}\in\argmin_{W\in\Theta_{1}}\bar{f}_{n}(W):=(1-w_{n})\bar{f}_{n-1}(W)+w_{n}\ell(X_{n},W).

If we have a target distribution
\pi
for the data matrices
X_{n}
, then the corresponding expected loss minimization problem is

(55)

\displaystyle W\in\argmin_{W\in\Theta_{1}}\,f(W):=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\pi}\left[\ell(X,W)\right].

The latter is known as the online matrix factorization problem introduced in
[
49
]
. A well-known instance is the online nonnegative matrix factorization, which corresponds to (
55
) with nonnegativity constraints for
W
and
H
.

In order to apply Algorithm
1
in this setting, denote
H_{n}\in\argmin_{H\in\Theta_{2}}\lVert X_{n}-W_{n-1}H\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda\lVert H\rVert_{1}
, which is an optimal code for factorizing
X_{n}
using the previous dictionary
W_{n-1}
. Then the function
g_{n}(W)=\lVert X_{n}-WH_{n}\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{1}
is a surrogate of
f_{n}:W\mapsto\ell(X_{n},W)
at
W_{n-1}
and belongs to
\surr_{L^{\prime\prime},0}(f_{n})
for some
L^{\prime\prime}>0
(see Ex.
B.6
.) A simple calculation shows that the resulting averaged surrogate function
\bar{g}_{n}
becomes

(56)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(W)=\textup{tr}(WA_{n}W^{T})-2\textup{tr}(WB_{n})+C_{n},

where the matrices
A_{n},B_{n},C_{n}
are recursively defined as

(57)

\displaystyle A_{n}=(1-w_{n})A_{n-1}+w_{n}H_{n}H_{n}^{T};\quad B_{n}=(1-w_{n})B_{n-1}+w_{n}H_{n}X_{n}^{T};

(58)

\displaystyle C_{n}=(1-w_{n})\bar{X}_{n-1}+w_{n}X_{n}X_{n}^{T}.

Thus Algorithm
1
in this case reduces to

(59)

\displaystyle\textit{Upon arrival of $X_{n}$:\hskip 10.22217pt}\begin{cases}H_{n}=\argmin_{H\in\Theta_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}}\lVert X_{n}-W_{n-1}H\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda\lVert H\rVert_{1}\\
A_{n}=(1-w_{n})A_{n-1}+w_{n}H_{n}H_{n}^{T}\\
B_{n}=(1-w_{n})B_{n-1}+w_{n}H_{n}X_{n}^{T}\\
W_{n}=\argmin_{W\in\Theta_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}}\left(\textup{tr}(WA_{n}W^{T})-2\textup{tr}(WB_{n})\right),\end{cases}

which is the online matrix factorization algorithm (OMF) proposed in
[
49
]
for i.i.d. data matrices.
Later in
[
42
]
, this algorithm was analyzed in a Markovian data setting. The following corollary is a direct consequence of our general results.

Corollary 5.4

(Rate of convergence of OMF)
.

Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
hold for the online matrix factorization algorithm (
59
) (case
C1
).

To the author’s best knowledge, such a result was not known for online matrix factorization even under the i.i.d. data setting.

5.1.4.
Subsampled Online Matrix Factorization

While the OMF algorithm (
59
) is efficient in handling matrices with a large number of columns
d
, its computational cost with respect to the data dimension
p
(i.e., the number of rows) is not reduced. In
[
50
]
, subsampled OMF was proposed to improve the computational efficiency with respect to
p
, by using only a random subsample of rows. For a preliminary version, consider the following variant of the online matrix factorization algorithm
59
, which uses a random coordinate descent on subsampled rows of
W_{n}
:

Upon arrival of
X_{n}
:

(60)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}H_{n}=\argmin_{H\in\Theta_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}}\lVert X_{n}-W_{n-1}H\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda\lVert H\rVert_{1}\\
A_{n}=(1-w_{n})A_{n-1}+w_{n}H_{n}H_{n}^{T}\\
B_{n}=(1-w_{n})B_{n-1}+w_{n}H_{n}X_{n}^{T}\\
J\leftarrow\textup{Random subset of $\{1,\dots,p\}$}\\
W_{n}=\argmin_{W\in\Theta_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}}\left(\textup{tr}(WA_{n}W^{T})-2\textup{tr}(WB_{n})\right)\\
\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{while freezing rows of $W_{n}$ not in $J$ to the rows of $W_{n-1}$}.\end{cases}

For instance,
J
may be taken as the uniform subset of
\{1,\dots,p\}
of a fixed size
p^{\prime}<p
or it may contain each index
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
independently with a fixed probability. All our main results in this paper (Theorems
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
) apply to the OMF algorithm in (
5.1.4
).

However, note that the code computation for
H_{n}
in (
5.1.4
) still involves solving a least squares problem with
p
dimensional data matrix
X_{n}
. In order to fully reduce the dependency on
p
, one may also use row subsampling to compute only
p^{\prime}
rows of
H_{n}
. The approach used in
[
50
]
was to replace
X_{n}
with an averaged
p^{\prime}
-dimenisonal matrix
\bar{X}_{n}^{(i)}
assuming
X_{n}=X^{(i)}
, where a finite pool of data matrices
\mathfrak{X}=\{X^{(1)},\dots,X^{(l)}\}
was assumed and
\bar{X}^{(i)}_{n}
is a recursively defined matrix of size
p^{\prime}\times d
based on previous occurances of the matrix
X^{(i)}
. See
[
50
, Alg. 3]
for more details.

An almost sure convergence to stationary points of subsampled OMF algorithm under i.i.d. data samples was shown in
[
50
]
. The analysis there is based on a general convergence result on stochastic approximate majorization-minimization algorithm (
7
) using strongly convex
\varepsilon
-approximate surrogate functions (see
[
50
, Prop. 3]
). A similar convergence result is retained by Theorem
4.1
, although the assumptions are slightly different. In addition, Theorems
4.2
-
4.3
also provide a rate of convergence, as stated in the following corollary.

Corollary 5.5

(Rate of convergence of Subsampled OMF)
.

Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
hold for the subsampled online matrix factorization algorithm (
5.1.4
) (case
C1
with randomized block coordinate descent).

5.1.5.
Online CP-dictionary Learning

Suppose we have
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{m}}
, which are
N
observed
m
-mode tensor-valued signals. Consider the following tensor-valued dictionary learning problem

(61)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{b}]\approx[\mathbf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{D}_{r}]\times_{m+1}H,

where
\times_{m+1}
denotes the mode-
(m+1)
tensor-matrix product and we impose the tensor dictionary atoms
\mathbf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{D}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{m}\times r}
to be of rank-1 and
H\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times b}
is called a code matrix. Equivalently, we assume that there exist
loading matrices

[U^{(1)},\dots,U^{(n)}]\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{I_{1}\times r}\times\dots\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{I_{n}\times r}
such that

(62)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{D}_{r}]

\displaystyle=\Out(U^{(1)},\dots,U^{(m)})

(63)

\displaystyle:=\left[\bigotimes_{k=1}^{m}U^{(k)}(:,1),\,\bigotimes_{k=1}^{m}U^{(k)}(:,2),\,\dots\,,\bigotimes_{k=1}^{m}U^{(k)}(:,r)\right]\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{m}\times r},

where
U^{(k)}(:,j)
denotes the
i^{\textup{th}}
column of the
I_{k}\times r
matrix
U^{(k)}
and
\otimes
denotes the outer product. Since we impose a CANDECOMP/PARAFAC (CP)
[
66
,
25
,
10
]
structure for the tensor-valued dictionary
[\mathbf{D}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{D}_{r}]
, the above is called a CP-dictionary learning problem introduced in
[
44
]
. When
m=1
, it reduces to the standard vector-valued dictionary learning problem.

In
[
44
]
, the following online CP-dictionary learning problem was proposed and analyzed. Fix compact and convex constraint sets for code and loading matrices
\mathcal{C}^{\textup{code}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{R\times b}
and
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{I_{i}\times r}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, respectively. Write
\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=\mathcal{C}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
. For each
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{n}\times b}
,
\mathbf{U}:=[U^{(1)},\dots,U^{(n)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{I_{1}\times r}\times\dots\times\mathbb{R}^{I_{n}\times r}
,
H\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times b}
, define

(64)

\displaystyle\ell(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{U},H)

\displaystyle:=\lVert\mathcal{X}-\Out(\mathbf{U})\times_{n+1}H\rVert_{F}^{2}+\lambda\lVert H\rVert_{1},

(65)

\displaystyle\ell(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{U})

\displaystyle:=\inf_{H\in\mathcal{C}^{\textup{code}}}\,\,\ell(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{U},H),

where
\lambda\geq 0
is a regularization parameter. Fix a sequence of non-increasing weights
(w_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
(0,1]
. Here
\mathcal{X}
denotes a minibatch of
b
tensors in
\mathbb{R}^{I_{1}\times\dots\times I_{n}}
, so minimizing
\ell(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{U})
with respect to
\mathbf{U}
amounts to fitting the CP-dictionary
\Out(\mathbf{U})
to the minibatch of
b
tensors in
\mathcal{X}
. The corresponding empirical loss minimization problem is

(66)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{n}\in\argmin_{\mathbf{U}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\bar{f}_{n}(\mathbf{U}):=(1-w_{n})\bar{f}_{n-1}(\mathbf{U})+w_{n}\ell(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathbf{U})\right).

If we have a target distribution
\pi
for the data tensors
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, then the corresponding expected loss minimization problem is

(67)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}\in\argmin_{\mathbf{U}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\,\left(f(W):=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\pi}\left[\ell(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{U})\right]\right).

In order to solve (
66
) and (
67
), the following online CP-dictionary learning algorithm was proposed and analyzed in
[
44
]
:

(68)

Upon arrival of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}H_{n}=\argmin_{H\in\mathcal{C}^{\textup{code}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r\times b}}\ell(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathbf{U}_{n-1},H)\\
\bar{g}_{n}(\mathbf{U})=(1-w_{n})\bar{g}_{n-1}(\mathbf{U})+w_{n}\ell(\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathbf{U},H_{n})\\
\textup{for $i=1,\dots,m$}:\\
\quad U_{n}^{(i)}\in\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}\\
\lVert U-U_{n-1}^{(i)}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}w_{n}\end{subarray}}\,\,\bar{g}_{n}(U_{n}^{(1)},\dots,U_{n}^{(i-1)},U,U_{n-1}^{(i+1)},\dots,U_{n-1}^{(m)}).\end{cases}

An almost sure convergence to the stationary points of the above algorithm under the Markovain data setting was obtained in
[
44
]
. In fact, the algorithm (
68
) is a special case of Algorithm
1
with
m
-block multi-convex variational surrogate corresponding (see Example
B.7
) to the function
\ell
in (
64
) with
\mathbb{J}=\{J_{1},\dots,J_{m}\}
, where
J_{i}
denotes the coordinate block corresponding to the
i
th loading matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{I_{i}\times r}
with cyclic block coordinate descent in Algorithm
3
.

Our generalized algorithm and refined analysis improve the results in
[
44
]
in multiple ways. First, Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
provide a rate of convergence results for the online CP-dictionary learning algorithm (
68
), which has not been known before.

Corollary 5.6

(Rate of convergence of OCPDL)
.

Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
hold for the online CP-dictionary learning algorithm (
68
) (case
C2
).

Second, due to the flexibility of using approximate surrogate functions, the convergence results of (
68
) hold under inexact code or factor matrix computation, following a similar argument as in
[
50
]
. Third, one can only optimize a small number of subsampled rows when updating the factor matrices
U_{n}^{(i)}
in (
68
) by using a refined block coordinate structure (see Example
B.8
). A similar idea of using subsampling for tensor CP-decomposition was also used recently in
[
35
]
.

Lastly, we remark that random row subsampling can be utilized for the code computation for
H_{n}
in (
68
), which essentially involves solving a least-squares problem with the mode-
(m+1)
unfolding of
\mathcal{X}_{n}
, which has size
(I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{m})\times b
. Since the number of rows of a such matrix
I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{m}
can be very large, the computational gain in this approach should be significant. We believe it would be straightforward to adapt the approach in
[
50
]
for subsampled online matrix factorization for a subsampled online CP-dictionary learning setting. For our theoretical results to apply, one only needs to verify that the resulting inexact code computation for
H_{n}
yields
\varepsilon_{n}
-approximate surrogates
\bar{g}_{n}
that verifies the assumption
(A4)
. We do not proceed with this line of research in the present paper.

5.2.
Experiments

In this section, we provide experimental results of SRMM on two tasks - Network Dictionary Learning
[
39
]
and Image classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks for the CIFAR-10 dataset
[
34
]
.

5.2.1.
Network Dictionary Learning

Network Dictionary Learning (NDL)
[
42
,
39
]
is the task of learning a fixed number of ‘latent motifs’ from a large number of connected
k
-node subgraphs in a given large and possibly sparse networks. The learned latent motifs provide a concise description of a network’s mesoscale structure and can be used for reconstructing and denoising networks (see
[
39
]
for more details). NDL is naturally formulated as a nonconvex and constrained stochastic optimization problem, where the input data is a stream of
k
-node subgraphs sampled by the MCMC motif-sampling algorithm
[
41
]
.

The standard optimization algorithm for NDL is based on SMM for online nonnegative matrix factorization (see Sec.
5.1.3
). We compare the performance of SMM (with adaptivity weights
w_{t}=1/t
) with that of various stochastic optimization algorithms – PSGD, PSGD with momentum (PSGD-HB) (with step size schedule
\alpha_{t}=1/t
) and AdaGrad
[
69
]
.

Figure 3.
Plot of reconstruction error vs. elapsed time for four algorithms for online NMF: AdaGrad, PSGD-Heavy Ball, PSGD, and SRMM. The data stream is a sequence of 4-node subgraph adjacency matrices sampled by an MCMC motif-sampling algorithm in
[
41
]
from three college Facebook networks
[
65
]
. The shaded region represents one standard deviation from ten runs.

We consider three facebook networks of schools
Caltech
,
UCLA
, and
Wisconsin
from the Facebook100 dataset
[
65
]
, following a similar setup to
[
42
]
.
We then used the MCMC motif-sampling algorithm of
[
41
]
to generate
300
correlated subgraphs from the networks. This gives a stream of 300
k\times k
binary subgraph adjacency matrices, from which the latent motifs need to be learned by solving an online nonnegative matrix factorization problem. We ran each stochastic optimization algorithm on this streaming data (in the order that each subgraph are sampled) exactly once (e.g., one epoch). We used four subgraph sizes:
k\in\{5,10,20,30\}
.

In Fig.
3
, we see the convergence of all the algorithms with respect to the normalized reconstruction error, which is in line with our theoretical results. We observe that SMM shows significantly faster convergence in all cases except the smallest subgraph size
k=4
is used, in which case AdaGrad seems to converge faster than all the other methods. Overall SMM seems to be providing robust performance for NDL with various choices of subgraph sizes.

5.2.2.
Image classification using CNN

Using DenseNet-121
[
26
]
and ResNet-34
[
29
]
, we then consider the task of image classification on the standard CIFAR-10 dataset
[
34
]
. We compared six different optimization algorithms: (1) The generalized double-averaging scheme (
45
) (labeled as SRMM); (2) The double-averaging scheme (
46
) (labeled as SMM); (3) SGD-HB (SGD with momentum); (4) AdaGrad
[
15
]
; (5) Adam
[
33
]
; and (6) AMSGrad
[
59
]
. We use these algorithms for a total of 130 epochs and report the train and test accuracies in Figure
4
. We see that SRMM achieves the highest train and test accuracies (around 93
\%
) the most rapidly both for DenseNet and ResNet. SMM also shows a competitive performance against the benchmark methods for DenseNet, and outperforms SGD-HB for ResNet in test accuracy.

Figure 4.
Plot of training and test accuracies for CIFAR-10 image classification using DenseNet-121 and ResNet-34 with various optimization algorithms – SRMM, SMM, SGD, AdaGrad, Adam, and AMSGrad.

In the experiments reported in Figure
4
, the hyperparameters are chosen as follows. (1) SRMM:
w_{n}=n^{-1/2}
for
n\geq 1
,
L\in\{10,1\}
, and
\lambda\in\{10,1,10^{-1},10^{-2},10^{-3}\}
; (2) SMM:
w_{n}=n^{-1/2}
for
n\geq 1
and
L\in\{10,1\}
; (3) SGD-HB: step sizes in
\{10^{-2},10^{-1},1,10,10^{2}\}
and momentum parameter of
0.9
; (4) AdaGrad: stepsizes in
\{\textup{5e-4},\textup{1e-3},\textup{5e-3},\textup{5e-2},\textup{1e-1}\}
and accumulator value 0; (5)-(6) Adam and AMSGrad: step sizes in
\{\textup{1e-4},\textup{5e-4},\textup{1e-3},\textup{5e-3},\textup{1e-2}\}
,
\beta_{1}\in\{0.9,0.99\}
, and
\beta_{2}\in\{0.99,0.999\}
. For SRMM and SMM, we reset the iteration counter to zero at the beginning of each epoch in order to avoid the adaptivity weight
w_{n}
becomes too small after many epochs.

6.
Preliminary analysis

In this section, we give some preliminary lemmas that we will use in our analysis in the following sections. Recall the definition of the optimality gaps
\Delta_{n}^{(i)}
in
(A5)
. Also, we assume throughout that
g_{n}\in\surr_{L,\rho}^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1},\varepsilon_{n})
for
n\geq 1
for some fixed set
\mathbb{J}
of coordinate blocks,
L>0
, and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
.

We first observe some regularity properties of the surrogate gradients
\nabla g_{n}
and
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume
(A1)
and
(A3)
. Suppose that the data sequence
(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is contained in some compact subset
\mathfrak{X}_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
. Then the following hold for all
n\geq 1
:

(i)

Both
\nabla g_{n}
and
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
are
L
-Lipschitz over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
;

(ii)

g_{n},\bar{g}_{n}
,
\nabla g_{n}
, and
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
are uniformly bounded over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
;

(iii)

There exists a constant
L^{\prime}>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,
g_{n},\bar{g}_{n}
are
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz continuous over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
and
\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert\leq L^{\prime}w_{n+1}
.

Proof.

Denote
f_{n}=\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\cdot)
for
n\geq 1
. Note that
\nabla\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
is
L
-Lipscthiz continuous (see
(A1)
). Also,
\nabla(g_{n}-f_{n})
is
L
-Lipscthiz by Definition
3.1
. It follows that
\nabla g_{n}
is also
L
-Lipschitz over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. This holds for all
n\geq 1
, so by an induction,
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
is also
L
-Lipschitz over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
for
n\geq 1
. This shows
(i)
.

Note that by Definition
3.1
,
\nabla(g_{n}-f_{n})
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous and has norm bounded by
\varepsilon_{n}\leq 1
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
. Since the parameter space
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is compact by
(A3)
, it follws that
\nabla(g_{n}-f_{n})
is uniformly bounded by some constant
L^{\prime}>0
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Also, by the assumption, the continuous map
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\mapsto\lVert\nabla\ell(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert
over the compact domain
\mathfrak{X}_{0}\times\boldsymbol{\Theta}
assumes bounded values. Hence
\nabla\ell
is uniformly bounded over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
by
(A1)
and
(A3)
. It follows that
\nabla f_{n}
is uniformly bounded, so
\nabla g_{n}
is also uniformly bonded. Then by induction, it follows that
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
is also uniformly bounded. Similarly, as
\lVert g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-f_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})\rVert\leq\varepsilon_{n}\leq 1
and
f_{n}
is uniformly bounded, it follows that
g_{n}
is bounded over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. By induction, it follows that
\bar{g}_{n}
is also uniformly bounded over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

To show
(iii)
, we first let
L^{\prime}>0
be a uniform bound on
\lVert\nabla g_{n}\rVert
and
\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}\rVert
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Fix
\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
and consider the linear curve
t\mapsto\gamma(t):=t\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}+(1-t)\boldsymbol{\theta}
in
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Then

(69)

\displaystyle\lVert g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})\rVert=\left|\int_{0}^{1}\langle\nabla g_{n}(\gamma(t)),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle\,dt\right|\leq L^{\prime}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert.

This shows that
g_{n}
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipscthiz for all
n\geq 1
. From this, one also concludes that
\bar{g}_{n}
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipscthiz for all
n\geq 1
.

Lastly, note that
\nabla\bar{g}_{n+1}=(1-w_{n+1})\nabla\bar{g}_{n}+w_{n+1}\nabla g_{n}
. Hence by
(ii)
, there exists
L^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
such that

(70)

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert\leq w_{n+1}\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert\leq L^{\prime}w_{n+1}

for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. This shows
(iii)
.
∎

Define a sequence
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
recursively as

(71)

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}=(1-w_{n})\bar{\varepsilon}_{n-1}+w_{n}\varepsilon_{n},\qquad\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}=0,

where
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is the sequence of surrogate error tolerance in Algorithm
1
. Then

(72)

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}w^{n}_{k}\varepsilon_{k}\leq w_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{k},

where
w^{n}_{k}
is defined in (
2
) and the inequality above uses
w^{n}_{1}\leq w^{n}_{2}\leq\dots\leq w^{n}_{n}=w_{n}
under
(A4)
. Since
g_{n}-f_{n}\geq-\varepsilon_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
, it follows that

(73)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}-\bar{f}_{n}\geq-\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}\geq-w_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{k}.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an output of Algorithm
1
. Assume
(A5)
. Then for all
n\geq 1
, the following hold:

(i)

(Forward Monotonicity)
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\geq\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)-\Delta_{n}
;

(ii)

(Stability of Estimates I)  Under case
C1
in Theorem
4.1
,
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
;

(iii)

(Stability of Estimates II)  Under case
C2
in Theorem
4.1
,
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}w_{n}
.

Proof.

First suppose Algorithm
2
is used for (
10
). By the definition of the optimality gap
\Delta_{n}
in
(A5)
,

(74)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})+\Delta_{n}.

On the other hand, suppose Algorithm
3
is used for (
10
). Consider the computation of
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
in Algorithm
3
. Let
J_{1},\dots,J_{m}\in\mathbb{J}
denote the coordinate blocks used in order, and let
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(1)},\dots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(m)}(=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
denote the outputs of (
13
) after each block minimization in Algorithm
3
. Denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
. Note that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}
is an approximate minimizer of the convex function
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
over the convex set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J_{i}}
. Also note that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J_{i}}
, by definition of the optimality gap
\Delta_{n}^{(i)}
in
(A5)
, for
1\leq i\leq m
,

(75)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)+\Delta_{n}^{(i)},

Summing over all
i=1,\dots,m
then gives
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\geq-\Delta_{n}
. Recall that in this case we take the regularizer
\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)
equals zero if
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}w_{n}/m
and
\infty
otherwise. Hence this shows
(i)
. Note that
(iii)
is trivial by the search radius restriction in Algorithm
1
.

It remains to show
(ii)
. We show the assertion under zero surrogate optimality gap
\Delta_{n}\equiv 0
(see
(A5)
) so that each
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
is the exact minimizer of the
\rho
-strongly convex function
\bar{g}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Indeed, by the second-order growth property (Lemma
C.4
) and using
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz continuity of
g_{n}
(see Lemma
6.1
), almost surely,

(76)

\displaystyle\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})

(77)

\displaystyle=(1-w_{n})\left(\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)+w_{n}\left(g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)

(78)

\displaystyle\leq w_{n}\left(g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)\leq w_{n}L^{\prime}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert.

This shows
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
, as desired. The proof for the general nonzero surrogate optimality gap can be found in Appendix
C
(see Lemma
C.6
).
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an output of Algorithm
1
. Assume
(A4)
. Then for each
n\geq 0
, the following hold:

(i)

\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\leq w_{n+1}\left(\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)+w_{n}^{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\varepsilon_{k}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Delta_{n+1}^{(i)}
.

(ii)

0\leq w_{n+1}\left(\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)\leq w_{n+1}\left(\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)+\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})+w_{n}^{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\varepsilon_{k}\right)
.

Proof.

We begin by observing that

(79)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle=(1-w_{n+1})\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+w_{n+1}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+w_{n+1}(g_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}))

for all
t\geq 0
. Hence

(80)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})

(81)

\displaystyle\quad=\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})

(82)

\displaystyle\quad=\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+(1-w_{n+1})\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+w_{n+1}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})

(83)

\displaystyle\quad=\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+w_{n+1}(\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}))+w_{n+1}(\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}))

(84)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+w_{n+1}(g_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})).

Now note that
\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Delta_{n+1}^{(i)}
by Lemma
6.2

(i)
. Also, recall that
\bar{g}_{n}-\bar{f}_{n}\geq-w_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{k}
from (
73
) and
0\leq g_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\leq\varepsilon_{n+1}
since
g_{n+1}
is a multi-convex
\varepsilon_{n}
-approximate surrogate of
f_{n+1}=\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\cdot)
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
. Thus the inequalities in both
(i)
and
(ii)
follow using
w_{n+1}\leq w_{n}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(b_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be sequences of nonnegative real numbers such that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}b_{n}<\infty
. Then the following hold.

(i)

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq k\leq n}b_{k}\leq\frac{\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}b_{k}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}}=O\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\right)^{-1}\right)
.

(ii)

Further assume
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}=\infty
and
|b_{n+1}-b_{n}|=O(a_{n})
. Then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}b_{n}=0
.

Proof.

(i)
follows from noting that

(85)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\right)\min_{1\leq k\leq n}b_{k}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}b_{k}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}b_{k}<\infty.

The proof of
(ii)
is omitted and can be found in
[
47
, Lem. A.5]
.
∎

7.
Key lemmas and proofs of main results

In this section, we state all key lemmas without proofs and derive the main results (Theorems
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
) assuming them. The key lemmas stated in this section will be proved in the subsequent sections.

7.1.
Key Lemmas

In this subsection, we state all key lemmas that are sufficient to derive the main results in this paper.

First, Lemma
7.1
states some general concentration inequalities of recursively defined functions similar to the empirical and the surrogate loss functions in (
1
) and (
8
). One may regard them as the classical Glivenko-Cantelli theorem for a general weighting scheme. We state the lemma in a self-contained manner so that it may be more convenient to be used for other purposes.

Lemma 7.1

(Uniform concentration of parameterized empirical observables)
.

Fix compact subsets
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and a bounded Borel measurable function
\psi:\mathcal{X}\times\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r}
. Let
(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
denote a sequence of points in
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{n}=\varphi(X_{n})
for
n\geq 1
, where
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a Markov chain on a state space
\Omega
and
\varphi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a measurable function. Fix a sequence of weights
w_{n}\in(0,1]
,
n\geq 0
and define functions
\bar{\psi}(\cdot):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x},\cdot)\right]
and
\bar{\psi}_{n}:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r}
recursively as
\bar{\psi}_{0}\equiv\mathbf{0}
and

(86)

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{n}(\cdot)=(1-w_{n})\bar{\psi}_{n-1}(\cdot)+w_{n}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{n},\cdot).

Assume the following:

(a1)

The Markov chain
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
mixes exponentially fast to its unique stationary distribution and the stochastic process
(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
on
\mathcal{X}
has a unique stationary distribution
\pi
.

(a2)

w_{n}
is non-increasing in
n
and
w_{n}^{-1}-w_{n-1}^{-1}\leq 1
for all sufficiently large
n\geq 1
.

Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(87)

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}[\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]\right\rVert\leq Cw_{n},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert\right]\leq Cw_{n}\sqrt{n}.

Furthermore, if
w_{n}\sqrt{n}=O(1/(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
almost surely.

We remark that a similar result appeared in
[
47
, Lem B.7]
, which states the second inequality in (
87
) as well as the last almost sure convergence statement under the stronger condition of
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}=\infty
and
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}<\infty
. We will refer to the latter condition as the ‘strong square-summability’ condition. These conditions were necessary in order to use Lemma
6.4

(ii)
to deduce the almost sure convergence. We give a direct argument using Borel-Cantelli lemma without this additional condition, for which the weaker condition of
w_{n}\sqrt{n}=O(1/(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
is sufficient.

Next, Lemma
7.2
states a series of finite variation statements that provide a basis for the forthcoming arguments. Most of the statements also appeared in the literature (see, e.g.,
[
49
,
47
,
42
,
44
]
) in some special cases, where the strong square summability condition was used. We give an improved argument only using the square summability condition except for the last item.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an output of Algorithm
1
under any of the three cases
C1
-
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. Suppose
(A1)
-
(A4)
. The following hold:

(i)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[w_{n+1}\left(\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)\right]^{+}<\infty
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]\right)^{+}<\infty
;

(iii)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|\right]<\infty
;

(iv)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]\right|<\infty
;

(v)

Suppose the optional condition in
(A4)
holds. Then
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left|f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|\right]<\infty
;

(vi)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty
.

The following lemma is one of the key innovations in the present work, which allows us to obtain convergence rate bounds stated in Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
. Roughly speaking, it gives gradient versions of the finite variation statements in Lemma
7.2
.

Lemma 7.3

(Variation of gradients)
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be the output of Algorithm
1
under any of the three cases
C1
-
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. Suppose
(A1)
-
(A4)
. The following hold:

(i)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\,\left(\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]\right\rVert^{2}\right)\right]<\infty.

(ii)

If in addition the optional condition in
(A4)
holds, then

(88)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\,\left(\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}+\lVert\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}\right)\right]<\infty.

Based on Lemmas
7.1
,
7.2
, and
7.3
, one can deduce Theorem
4.2
as well as Theorem
4.1
for case
C1
. A nice property of Algorithm
1
with convex surrogates
g_{n}
with identically zero regularization (
\Psi_{n}\equiv 0
in (
10
)) and optimality gaps (see
(A5)
) is that the iterates
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
are exact minimizers of the convex averaged surrogates
\bar{g}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Hence
-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
lies in the normal cone of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
for each
n\geq 1
. Thus, in order for asymptotic stationarity with respect to the empirical loss
\bar{f}_{n}
or the expected loss
f
, one only needs to show that every convergent subsequence of
\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla h_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert
vanish almost surely, where
h_{n}\in\{\bar{f}_{n},f\}
for
n\geq 1
.

However, when
\bar{g}_{n}
is (approximately) minimized with proximal regularization (Algorithm
2
) or within a diminishing radius (Algorithm
3
),
-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
may be close to but not within the normal cone of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
. For instance, even though each convex sub-problem in Algorithm
3
is exactly solved, the technique of radius restriction in Algorithm
3
introduces additional radius constraints so that
-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
may be normal to the trust region boundary, which is the sphere of distance
c^{\prime}w_{n}/m
centered at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
; Similarly, using proximal regularization in Algorithm
2
tilts the surrogate gradient. In order to handle these issues, in Lemma
7.4
, we will show that the sequence
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
,
n\geq 1
still verifies stationarity for
\bar{g}_{n}
in an asymptotic sense. For this, we need to take ‘convergent subsequences‘ of the averaged surrogate functions
\bar{g}_{n}
, which can be easily done under the compact parameterization assumption in
(A7)
.

Lemma 7.4

(Asymptotic Surrogate Stationarity)
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
and
(A7)
and let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an output of Algorithm
1
under any of the three cases
C1
-
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. Assume that
\Delta_{n}=o(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)
. Let
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
and
\bar{g}_{t_{k}}
converges almost surely. Then
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
is almost surely a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{t_{k}}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

Finally, Lemma
7.5
is the last ingredient for proving Theorem
4.3
, which states that the optimality for the averaged surrogates
\bar{g}_{n}
is summable with weights
w_{n}
.

Lemma 7.5

(Finite variation of surrogate optimality)
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an output of Algorithm
1
under any of the three cases
C1
-
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
. Then for each initialization
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{0}
, we have

(89)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\right]<\infty.

7.2.
Proof of Theorem
4.2

In this subsection, we will assume Lemmas
7.1
,
7.2
, and
7.3
and prove Theorem
4.2
.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Suppose
(A1)
-
(A4)
and any of the cases
C1
-
C3
stated in Theorem
4.1
. By Lemma
7.2

(iii)
and Lemma
7.3

(i)
, we get

(90)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left(\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}\right)\right]<\infty.

Recall that
\mathbb{E}[|X|]<\infty
implies
|X|<\infty
almost surely for any random variable
X
. It follows that the summation above is almost surely finite. Then (
24
) follows from Lemma
6.4
.

Next, we show (
26
). First we deduce some intermediate bounds. By Lemma
7.2

(iii)
-
(iv)
and Jensen’s inequality, we get

(91)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\left|\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]\right|\right]

(92)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|\right]+\left|\mathbb{E}[\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]\right|\right)\right]<\infty.

Similarly, using Lemma
7.3

(i)
, we can deduce

(93)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\,\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]\right\rVert^{2}<\infty.

Now recall that by Lemma
7.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(94)

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lvert f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}[\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]\right\rvert\leq Cw_{n},\qquad\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}[\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]\right\rVert\leq Cw_{n}.

Using (
91
) and the first bound in (
94
), we get

(95)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]\rvert+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rvert\right)^{2}

(96)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]\right\rvert^{2}+Cw_{n+1}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]\right\rvert+C^{2}w_{n+1}^{2}.

Recalling that
\bar{g}_{n}
and
\bar{f}_{n}
are uniformly bounded over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
by
(A1)
, using (
91
) we get

(97)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right\rvert^{2}\right]<\infty.

An identical argument using (
93
) as wel as the second bound in (
94
) shows

(98)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty.

Combining the above two bounds and using Lemma
6.4
give (
26
).

It remains to show (
25
) and (
27
). Suppose the optional condition in
(A4)
holds. Then by Lemma
7.2

(v)
and Lemma
7.3

(ii)
, we deduce

(99)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left(\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}\right)\right]<\infty.

Then (
27
) follows from Lemma
6.4
.

Lastly, recall that by Lemma
7.1
and see
(A4)
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(100)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lvert f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rvert\right]\leq Cw_{n}\sqrt{n}\leq 2C,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert\right]\leq Cw_{n}\sqrt{n}\leq 2C.

Then note that

(101)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rvert^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]\rvert+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\,\lvert\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rvert\right)^{2}\right]

(102)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right\rvert^{2}+2C\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\mathbb{E}[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]\right\rvert\right]+C^{2}(n+1)w_{n+1}^{2}.

Then multiply
w_{n}
on both sides sum over all
n\geq 1
. The first two terms in the resulting infinite sum in the righthand side are finite by (
97
) and (
91
). For the last term, note that since
w_{n}\sqrt{n}=o(1)
(see
(A4)
) and using the optional condition in
(A4)
,

(103)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(n+1)w_{n+1}^{3}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(w_{n+1}\sqrt{n+1}\right)w_{n+1}^{2}\sqrt{n+1}\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}^{2}\sqrt{n+1}<\infty

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
. It follows that

(104)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right\rvert^{2}\right]<\infty.

An identical argument using the second bound in (
100
) as well as (
93
) shows

(105)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty.

Combining the above two bounds gives (
25
). This completes the proof.
∎

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Next, we prove Theorem
4.1
assuming Lemmas
7.1
,
7.2
,
7.3
, and
7.4
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Suppose
(A1)
-
(A4)
. First assume cases
C1
or
C2
in Theorem
4.1
. We will first show the assertion except for the asymptotic stationarity statements.

We first show the first part of
(i)
. By Lemma
7.2

(iii)
and Lemma
7.3

(i)
, we get

(106)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left(\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}\right)\right]<\infty.

Denote
h^{(1)}_{n}:=\bar{g}_{n}-\bar{f}_{n}
and
h^{(2)}_{n}:=\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}\rVert^{2}
. We claim that
|h^{(i)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-h^{(i)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|=O(w_{n+1})
for
i=1,2
. Then by Lemma
6.4
, it holds that
|h^{(i)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|\rightarrow 0
almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
for
i=1,2
.

To show the claim, recall the recursive definitions of
\bar{g}_{n}
and
\bar{f}_{n}
and that
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\ell(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})
is
R
-Lipschitz continuous by
(A1)
for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Also,
h^{(1)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=(1-w_{n})h^{(1)}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+w_{n}(g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}))
, where
f_{n}(\cdot)=\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n},\cdot)
and
g_{n}\in\surr_{L,\rho}^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})
(see Definition
3.1
) and
\nabla(g_{n}-f_{n})
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous and has norm
\leq\varepsilon_{n}
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
. Since the parameter space
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is compact by
(A3)
, it follws that
\nabla(g_{n}-f_{n})
is uniformly bounded by some constant
L^{\prime}>0
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Then
g_{n}-f_{n}
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz, and by induction,
h^{(1)}_{n}
is also
L
-Lipschitz for
n\geq 1
. Now note that

(107)

\displaystyle|h^{(1)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-h^{(1)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq|h^{(1)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-h^{(1)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|+|h^{(1)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-h^{(1)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|

(108)

\displaystyle\leq L^{\prime}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert+\left|\left(\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)-\left(\bar{f}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)\right|

(109)

\displaystyle\leq L^{\prime}c^{\prime}w_{n}+w_{n+1}\left|g_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-f_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|

(110)

\displaystyle=L^{\prime}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert+w_{n+1}\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|,

where the third inequality uses Lemma
6.2

(iii)
as well as the recursive definitions of
\bar{g}_{n+1}
and
\bar{f}_{n+1}
. Then note that
\ell:\mathcal{X}\times\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is bounded by
(A1)
, so
\bar{g}_{n}
and
\bar{f}_{n}
are also uniformly bounded in
n
. This shows
|h^{(1)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-h^{(1)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|=O(w_{n+1})
.

Next, we verify
|h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})|=O(w_{n+1})
. Note that by linearity of gradients,
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
and
\nabla\bar{f}_{n}
satisfy the same recursion as
\bar{g}_{n}
and
\bar{f}_{n}
. Moreover,
\nabla h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=(1-w_{n})\nabla h^{(2)}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+w_{n}\nabla(g_{n}-f_{n})(\boldsymbol{\theta})
and since
\nabla(g_{n}-f_{n})
is
L
-Lipschitz, so is
\nabla h^{(2)}_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. Then by using a similar argument as above,

(111)

\displaystyle\left|\sqrt{h^{(2)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})}-\sqrt{h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})}\right|

(112)

\displaystyle\qquad=\lVert\nabla h_{n+1}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-\nabla h_{n+1}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert+\lVert\nabla h_{n+1}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla h_{n}^{(2)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert

(113)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq L\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert+w_{n+1}\lVert\nabla(g_{n+1}-f_{n+1})(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert

(114)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq Lc^{\prime}w_{n+1}+w_{n+1}\lVert\nabla h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert.

But recall that
\nabla h^{(2)}_{n}
is uniformly bounded by
L^{\prime}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. To conclude, write

(115)

\displaystyle|h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})|=\left|\sqrt{h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})}-\sqrt{h^{(2)}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})}\right|\cdot\left|\sqrt{h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})}+\sqrt{h^{(2)}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})}\right|.

Noting that
h^{(2)}_{n}
is uniformly bounded for all
n\geq 1
, we conclude that
|h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-h^{(2)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})|=O(w_{n+1})
, as desired.

Next, we show the first part of
(ii)
. Recall that under
(A4)
(without the optional condition), by Lemma
7.1
(se also Lemma
C.9
), we have
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}|\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})|\rightarrow 0
and
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\lVert\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert\rightarrow 0
almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Then by
(i)
, we have

(116)

\displaystyle|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|\leq|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}|\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})|\rightarrow 0\quad\textup{a.s. as $n\rightarrow\infty$}.

Similarly, by triangle inequality and
(i)
,

(117)

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert\leq\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\lVert\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert\rightarrow 0\quad\textup{a.s. as $n\rightarrow\infty$}.

Now we show the second parts of
(i)
and
(ii)
, the asymptotic stationarity. Assume case
C1
. Then each
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
is an exact minimizer of
\bar{g}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, so
-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
is in the normal cone of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
for each
n\geq 1
. Since we have shown that
\lVert\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert
and
\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert
both converges to zero almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
, it follows that both
-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
and
-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
belong to the normal cone of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
asymptotically almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Assume case
C2
. Further assume
(A7)
. Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
be an arbitrary limit point of the sequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and let
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a (random) sequence such that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Since
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\times\mathcal{K}
is compact, we may choose a further sequence of
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
, which we will denote the same, so that
\kappa_{t_{k}}
converges to some element
\kappa_{\infty}\in\mathcal{K}
. Hence
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{t_{k}}
is well-defined almost surely. It is important to note that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
by Lemma
7.4
. Hence
-\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
is in the normal cone of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
. But since we have shown that
h^{(i)}\rightarrow 0
almost suresly as
n\rightarrow\infty
for
i=2,4
, we must have

(118)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\nabla\bar{f}_{t_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})=\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})=\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}).

Since
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
was an arbitrary limit point of
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, this completes the proof of
(i)
-
(ii)
.

Lastly, assume
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. In this case, we do not have iterate stability
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
so we cannot use Lemma
6.4

(ii)
to deduce the whole sequence convergence as we did before. However, we can still deduce subsequential convergence. Indeed, by Lemma
7.2

(iii)
and Lemma
7.3

(i)
, we have (
106
). Then by Lemma
6.4

(i)
and noting that
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}=\infty
, we conclude that there exists a subsequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})_{k\geq 1}
of
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that almost surely,
\left|\bar{g}_{n_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})-\bar{f}_{n_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})\rVert^{2}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Also, using a similar argument as before in and , we can deduce that almost surely along the same subsequence,
\left|\bar{g}_{n_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}})\rVert^{2}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Lastly, asymptotic stationarity for
\bar{g}_{n}
is given by Lemma
7.4
.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

Without appealing to Lemma
7.1
and using the convergence results for the empirical loss minimization stated in Theorem
4.1
, one can directly prove Theorem
4.1

(ii)
using a similar argument as in the proof of
(i)
. This amounts to use the finite sum (
99
) in place of (
90
) and showing the bounds
|h^{(i)}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})-h^{(i)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})|=O(w_{n+1})
for
i=3,4
, where
h^{(3)}=\bar{g}_{n+1}-f
and
h^{(4)}_{n}=\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}-\nabla f\rVert^{2}
. However, (
99
) holds under the additional optional condition in
(A4)
, which essentially says
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}<\infty
. While this condition is standard in the literature (see, e.g.,
[
47
, (E)]
),
[
42
, (M2)]
, and
[
44
, (A3)]
, our proof above avoids it and only relies on the more standard square summability condition
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\log n)w^{2}<\infty
(see
(A4)
). However, the stronger condition
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}<\infty
as well as the finite sum (
99
) is crucial in our proof of Theorem
4.3
.

7.4.
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Next, we prove Theorem
4.3
assuming Lemmas
7.2
,
7.3
, and
7.5
.

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

We first show
(i)
. Note that (
28
) follows immediately from Lemmas
7.5
and
6.4
. Next, we show (
29
). By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
,

(119)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle-\left\langle\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\leq\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert.

It follows that for all
n\geq 1
,

(120)

\displaystyle\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\leq\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert.

On the other hand, by Lemmas
7.5
and
7.3

(i)
, we have

(121)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left[\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty.

Then by Lemma
6.4
, we have

(122)

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq k\leq n}\left[\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})-\nabla\bar{f}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\rVert^{2}\right]=O\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}\right)^{-1}\right).

Let
t_{n}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
for
n\geq 1
be such that the minimum above is achieved. Namely, denoting the term in the minimum above by
A_{k}
, we have
A_{t_{n}}=O\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}\right)^{-1}\right)
. Since all terms in
A_{k}
are nonnegative, it follows that there exists a constant
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
, alsmot surely,

(123)

\displaystyle\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{c_{1}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}},\quad\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})\rVert\leq\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}}}.

Hence from (
120
), it follows that there exists some constant
c_{3}>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(124)

\displaystyle\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{f}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{c_{3}}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}}}.

This completes the proof of
(i)
.

Next, we show (
30
) in
(ii)
. Recall that by Lemma
7.1
and see
(A4)
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(125)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert\right]\leq Cw_{n}\sqrt{n}.

Then first observe that by Cauchy-Schwarz and triangle inequalities,

(126)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle-\left\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert

(127)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\lVert\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert.

It follows that for all
n\geq 1
,

(128)

\displaystyle\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert

(129)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\lVert\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert.

Note that by Lemmas
7.5
,
7.3

(i)
, we have

(130)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}\right]\right)<\infty.

By Lemma
6.4
, similarly as before, we can take a sequence
t_{n}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
for
n\geq 1
such that for some constants
c_{3},c_{4}>0
and for all
n\geq 1
,

(131)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\right]\leq\frac{c_{3}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})\rVert\right]\leq\frac{c_{4}}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}}}.

Hence taking expectation on (
128
) and using this sequence
t_{n}
with (
125
), we get

(132)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\right]\leq\frac{c_{5}}{\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}}}+w_{n}\sqrt{n}.

for all
n\geq 1
for some constant
c_{5}>0
. This shows (
30
).

It remains to show (
31
) in
(ii)
. Assume the optional condition in
(A4)
holds. Using a similar argument as before, for all
n\geq 1
,

(133)

\displaystyle\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\leq\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert.

Also, by Lemmas
7.5
and
7.3

(ii)
, almost surely,

(134)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left[\left|-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty.

The rest of the argument is identical to the proof of
(i)
.
∎

7.5.
Proofs of Corollaries
4.4
and
4.5

Lastly in this section, we prove Corollaries
4.4
and
4.5
.

Proof of Corollary
4.4
.

Suppose the iterates
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
are all in the interior of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Then in Theorem
4.3
(
28
), we may choose
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
in a way that
\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
is a positive scalar multiple of
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
. Then (
28
) reduces to the first bound in (
32
). The same argument shows the second bound in (
32
). For the last bound in (
32
), we use (
98
) we derived in the proof of Theorem
4.2
. Combining it with Lemma
7.5
and using Fubini’s theorem, we get

(135)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert\right]+\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})]-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right\rVert^{2}\right)<\infty.

Then by Lemma
6.4
, we have

(136)

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq k\leq n}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\rVert\right]+\left\lVert\nabla\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})]-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k})\right\rVert^{2}\right]=O\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}w_{k}\right)^{-1}\right)

Choose a subsequence
t_{n}
along which the above minimum is achieved for all
n\geq 1
. Then we have

(137)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})\rVert\right]=O\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t_{n}}w_{k}\right)^{-1}\right),\quad\left\lVert\nabla\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})]-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})\right\rVert=O\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t_{n}}w_{k}\right)^{-1/2}\right).

Therefore by using Jensen’s inequality and the above bound, we deduce

(138)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})\right\rVert\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})\right\rVert\right]+\left\lVert\nabla\mathbb{E}[\bar{g}_{t_{n}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})]-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{n}})\right\rVert=O\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t_{n}}w_{k}\right)^{-1/2}\right).

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.5

.

First, suppose
w_{n}=n^{-1/2}(\log n)^{\delta}
for some
\delta>1
. Then the upper bound on the rate of convergence in Theorem
4.3
is of order
O(n^{-1/2}(\log n)^{\delta})
. Then one can conclude by using the fact that
n\geq\varepsilon^{-2}(3\log\varepsilon^{-1})^{2\delta}
implies
n^{-1/2}(\log n)^{\delta}\leq\varepsilon
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. Indeed, assuming
n\geq\varepsilon^{-2}(3\log\varepsilon^{-1})^{2\delta}
,

(139)

\displaystyle n^{-1/2}(\log n)^{\delta}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{(3\log\varepsilon^{-1})^{\delta}}(2\log\varepsilon^{-1}+2\delta\log(3\log\varepsilon^{-1}))^{\delta}

and the last expression is at most
\varepsilon
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. This shows
(i)
. A similar argument using Theorem
4.3

(
ii) as well as Corollary
4.4
shows
(ii)
.
∎

8.
Proof of Lemmas
7.1
,
7.2
, and
7.3

Recall that for each
n\geq 0
,
\mathcal{F}_{n}
denotes the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the history of underlying Markov chain
Y_{0},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
as well as the possible randomness in Algorithm
1
up to iteration
n
. Note that
\mathbf{x}_{n}=\varphi(Y_{n})
for
n\geq 1
by
(A2)
.

We first prove Lemma
7.1
below. Our proof uses an auxiliary lemma, Lemma
C.9
in the appendix.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Recall that
\mathbf{x}_{k}=\varphi(Y_{k})
under
(A2)
. Let
\pi_{k}
denote the distribution of
Y_{k}
. Let
M:=\sup_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta}}\lVert\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\rVert<\infty
. Note that, by a change of measure,

(140)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega}\psi(\varphi(\mathbf{y}),\boldsymbol{\theta})\,P^{n}(Y_{0},\mathbf{y})

(141)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega}\psi(\varphi(\mathbf{y}),\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\pi(\mathbf{y})+\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega}\psi(\varphi(Y_{0}),\boldsymbol{\theta})(P^{n}(Y_{0},\mathbf{y})-\pi(\mathbf{y}))

(142)

\displaystyle=\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega}\psi(\varphi(Y_{0}),\boldsymbol{\theta})\,(P^{n}(Y_{0},\mathbf{y})-\pi(\mathbf{y})).

By the triangle inequality, it follows that

(143)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x}_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega}\left\lVert\psi(\varphi(Y_{0}),\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert\,\,\left|P^{n}(Y_{0},\mathbf{y})-\pi(\mathbf{y})\right|

(144)

\displaystyle\leq M\sum_{y\in\Omega}\left|P^{n}(Y_{0},\mathbf{y})-\pi(\mathbf{y})\right|

(145)

\displaystyle\leq 2M\lVert P^{n}(Y_{0},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV},

where the last inequality follows from the relation
2\lVert\mu-\nu\rVert_{TV}=\sum_{x}|\mu(x)-\nu(x)|
where
\mu,\nu
are probability distributions on the same sample space (see
[
43
, Prop. 4.2]
).

Now recall that under the hypothesis in
(A4)
,
w^{n}_{k}=w_{k}\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}(1-w_{i})
is non-decreasing in
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. So,
