w^{n}_{1}\leq\dots\leq w^{n}_{n}=w_{n}
.
Then using
(A2)
, we have

(146)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{\psi}_{n}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}[\psi(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})]\right)w^{n}_{k}\right\rVert

(147)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}[\psi(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})]\right\rVert w^{n}_{k}

(148)

\displaystyle\leq 2M\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lVert P^{k}(Y_{0},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}w^{n}_{k}

(149)

\displaystyle\leq 2Mw_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathfrak{X}}\,\lVert P^{k}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}\leq\frac{2L\lambda w_{n}}{1-\lambda}.

This shows the first bound in (
87
).

Finally, for
d=1
, the second inequality in (
87
), as well as the last part of the statement, are direct consequences of Lemma
C.9
, nothing that
w^{n}_{1}\leq\dots\leq w^{n}_{n}=w_{n}
. Applying this to each of the
d
coordinates of
\psi
and applying triangle inequality will imply the assertion. Namely, write
\psi=(\psi^{(1)},\dots,\psi^{(d)})^{T}
. Then noting that
\psi,\bar{\psi}_{n}
are uniformly bounded by some constant
M>0
, we have

(150)

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lvert\bar{\psi}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}^{(i)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rvert^{2}\leq 2M\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lvert\bar{\psi}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}^{(i)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rvert.

Note that by Lemma
C.9
, the expectation of each summand in the last expression is of order
O(w_{n}\sqrt{n})
. Hence the second inequality in (
87
) follows. Lastly, the summands in the last expression above converge to zero almost surely if
w^{n}_{n}\sqrt{n}=O(1/(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
by Lemma
C.9
. Note that
w^{n}_{n}=w_{n}
. Hence the left-hand side above converges to zero almost surely
w_{n}\sqrt{n}=O(1/(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})
. This completes the proof.
∎

To handle the issue of dependence in signals, we adopt the strategy developed in
[
42
]
in order to handle a similar issue for vector-valued signals (or matrix factorization). The key insight in
[
42
]
is that, while the 1-step conditional distribution
P(Y_{t-1},\cdot)
may be far from the stationary distribution
\pi
, the
N
-step conditional distribution
P^{N}(Y_{t-N},\cdot)
is exponentially close to
\pi
under mild conditions. Hence we can condition much early on – at time
t-N
for some suitable
N=N(t)
. Then the Markov chain runs
N+1
steps up to time
t+1
, so if
N
is large enough for the chain to mix to its stationary distribution
\pi
, then the distribution of
Y_{t+1}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{t-N}
is close to
\pi
. The error of approximating the stationary distribution by the
N+1
step distribution can be controlled using total variation distance and Markov chain mixing bound. We refine the analysis for
[
42
, Lem. 12]
and obtain a more improved version below.

Proposition 8.1
.

Assumptions
(A1)
-
(A4)
hold. Let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an output of Algorithm and denote
L=\lVert\ell(\cdot,\cdot)\rVert_{\infty}
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
0\leq N\leq n
, it holds that

(151)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]^{+}\right]\leq Cw_{n-N}+2L\lambda^{N+1},

where
\lambda\in[0,1)
is the exponential mixing rate of the underlying Markov chain in
(A2)
. Furthermore, if
\mathbf{x}_{n}
’s are i.i.d. from
\pi
, then the assertion holds with
N=0
and
\lambda=0
.

Proof.

Fix
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega
and suppose
Y_{n-N}=\mathbf{y}
. By the Markov property, the distribution of
Y_{n+1}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
equals
P^{N+1}(Y_{n-N},\cdot)
, where
P
denotes the transition kernel of the chain
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Then by the Markov property, the distribution of
Y_{n+1}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
equals
P^{N+1}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)
, where
P
denotes the transition kernel of the chain
(Y_{n})_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
. Denote
L:=\lVert\ell(\cdot,\cdot)\rVert_{\infty}
. Denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N}
, which is deterministic with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
.

We first claim that

(152)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\leq 2L\lVert P^{N+1}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}\leq 2L\lambda^{N+1}.

Then this would yield

(153)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]\leq f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\leq f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+2L\lambda^{N+1}.

To justify (
152
), note that by a change of measure,

(154)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\ell(\varphi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{\theta})\,P^{N+1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})

(155)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\ell(\varphi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\pi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})+\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\ell(\varphi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{\theta})(P^{N+1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\pi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}))

(156)

\displaystyle=f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\ell(\varphi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{\theta})(P^{N+1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\pi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})).

Then the claim follows by bouding
\ell(\varphi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{\theta})\leq\lVert\cdot,\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert_{\infty}
for all
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega
and
\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega}\left|P^{N+1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})-\pi(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})\right|=2\lVert P^{N+1}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}
.

Next, we analyze
\mathbb{E}\left[-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]
using a different approach. Namely, we decompose the times interval
[0,n]
into two intervals
[1,n-N]
and
[n-N+1,t]
and consider the trajectory
(\mathbf{x}_{k})_{1\leq k\leq n}
restricted onto each of these intervals. Since we are conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
, the trajectory
(\mathbf{x}_{k})_{1\leq k\leq n-N}
is fully observed and may be far from the average behavior. The distribution of
\mathbf{x}_{k}
then becomes close to the stationary distribution
\pi
during the second interval of length at an exponential mixing rate of
\lambda
(see
(A2)
). We first compare the trajectory on the second interval using the Markov chain mixing, and then compare the mean of the trajectory of the first interval with the stationary trajectory by integrating out the conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
. See Figure
5
for illustration.

Figure 5.
Illustration of the decomposition of the process used to bound the positive variation.

We proceed with the sketch given above. Denote
\hat{f}_{n:N}:=\sum_{k=t-N+1}^{n}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\cdot)w^{n}_{k}
. Write

(157)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})w^{n}_{k}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]=-\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n-N}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})w^{n}_{k}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{f}_{n:N}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

Next, denote
C_{N}=\sum_{k=n-N+1}^{n}w^{n}_{k}
. Using Markov property, we have

(158)

\displaystyle\left|C_{N}f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{f}_{n:N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=t-N+1}^{n}\left|f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}\sim\pi_{k-N}}[\ell(\varphi(\mathbf{y}),\boldsymbol{\theta})]\right|w^{n}_{k}

(159)

\displaystyle\leq 2L\sum_{k=1}^{n-N}\lVert P^{k}(Y_{n-N},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}w^{n}_{k}

(160)

\displaystyle\leq 2Lw_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathfrak{X}}\,\lVert P^{k}(\mathbf{y},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}\leq\frac{2Lw_{n}}{1-\lambda},

by using
w^{n}_{1}\leq\dots\leq w^{n}_{n}=w_{n}\leq 1
under
(A4)
and also
(A2)
to bound the total variation distance terms. Hence by triangle inequality,

(161)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

\displaystyle\leq-\sum_{k=1}^{n-N}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})w^{n}_{k}-C_{N}f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\mathbb{E}\left[\left|C_{N}f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\hat{f}_{n:N}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

(162)

\displaystyle\leq-\sum_{k=1}^{n-N}\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})w^{n}_{k}-\sum_{k=n-N+1}^{n}f(\boldsymbol{\theta})w^{n}_{k}+\frac{2L}{1-\lambda}.

Then combining the above bounds with (
152
) and a triangle inequality gives

(163)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]\right)^{+}

(164)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|+\mathbb{E}\left[-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

(165)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n-N}\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)w^{n}_{k}\right)+\frac{2Lw_{n}}{1-\lambda}+2L\lambda^{N+1}

(166)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n-N}\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)w^{n-N+M}_{k}\right)+\frac{2Lw_{n-N}}{1-\lambda}+2L\lambda^{N+1},

where we have used that
w_{k}
is non-increasing in
k
and
w^{n}_{k}=w_{k}\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}(1-w_{i})\leq w_{k}\prod_{i=k+1}^{m}(1-w_{i})=w^{m}_{k}
for
k\leq m\leq n
. Then by Lemma
7.1
, we have

(167)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n-N+M}\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\ell(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)w^{n-N+M}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq Cw_{n-N}

Therefore, the assertion follows from (
161
) by integrating it with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
and using (
167
).

Lastly, suppose the data sequence
\mathbf{x}_{n}
is i.i.d. from the stationary distribution
\pi
. We can then take
Y_{n}=\mathbf{x}_{n}
, so
Y_{n}
is a Markov chain with mixing rate
\lambda=1
, since for any
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega
, the one-step conditional distribution
P(\mathbf{y},\cdot)
exactly equals the stationary distribution
\pi
. This shows the assertion.
∎

Next, we give proof of Lemma
7.2
.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

We will first show
(i)
-
(v)
under cases
C1
-
C2
in Theorem
4.1
. We will then show these statements for case
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. After that, we will finally prove
(vi)
.

Denote
V(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\ell(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. By
(A1)
,
V
is Lipschitz in
\boldsymbol{\theta}
for some
R>0
. Under cases
C1
-
C2
, by Lemma
6.2
, we have
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k-1}\rVert\leq c_{0}w_{k}
for all
k\geq 1
for some constant
c_{0}>0
. Since
w_{k}
is non-increasing in
k
, by triangle inequality we get
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\leq c_{0}\sum_{k=n-N}^{N}w_{k}\leq c_{0}Nw_{n-N}
. This yields

(168)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

(169)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]+R\,\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

(170)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]+c_{0}RNw_{n-N}.

Note that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N}
is deterministic with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
. Hence multiplying by
w_{n+1}\leq w_{n}
, taking positive parts, and using Proposition
8.1
, we get

(171)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[w_{n+1}V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-a_{n}}\right]^{+}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}Cw_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}+2Lw_{n}\lambda^{a_{n}+1}+c_{0}Ra_{n}w_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}.

Now we show
(i)
. Denote
Z_{n}=w_{n+1}V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
. Then by iterated expectation and Jensen’s inequality, it follows that

(172)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[Z_{n}]^{+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{n}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-a_{t}}\right]\right]\right)^{+}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{t}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{t-a_{t}}\right]\right)^{+}\right].

Notice that the first expression above is the summation in
(i)
that we want to bound, and the last expression above equals the left-hand side of (
171
). This shows
(i)
.

Next, we show
(ii)
. Note that the left hand side in
(ii)
is bounded by the left hand side of
(i)
plus
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{k}]\right)<\infty
by Proposition
6.3
and
(A4)
. So
(ii)
follows from
(i)
.

For
(iii)
, observe from Proposition
6.3
and (
73
),

(173)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left(\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)\leq\bar{g}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\left(\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}\varepsilon_{k}\right).

Then
(iii)
follows from taking expectation and using
(i)
with
(A4)
.

Next, we show
(iv)
. The argument is similar. Since the loss function
\ell
is
R
-Lipstchitz by
(A1)
, so is
f-\bar{f}_{n}
. Then, as before, we can
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq c_{0}w_{n}
and
w_{n}
non-increasing to show

(174)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})-\bar{f}_{n-N}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]\right|+c_{0}RNw_{n-N}.

Note that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N}
is deterministic with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
. Hence using Proposition
8.1
with
N=a_{n}
where
a_{n}
as in
(A4)
, it follows that

(175)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-a_{n}}\right]\right|\leq\frac{2Lw_{n-a_{n}}}{1-\lambda}+c_{0}Ra_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}.

Multiplying by
w_{n+1}
and integrating with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n-a_{n}}
, we get

(176)

\displaystyle w_{n+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathbb{E}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-a_{n}}\right]\right|\right]\leq\frac{2Lw_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}}{1-\lambda}+c_{0}Ra_{n}w_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}.

By
(A4)
, the right-hand side is summable. Then the assertion follows from Jensen’s inequality similarly as in the proof of
(i)
.

We turn to prove
(v)
. Suppose the optional condition in
(A4)
holds, that is,
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}\sqrt{n}<\infty
, where
a_{n}
is the sequence in
(A4)
. As in the proof of
(iv)
, and using the second inequality in Lemma
7.1
,

(177)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-N})-\bar{f}_{n-N}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-N}\right]+c_{0}RNw_{n-N}

(178)

\displaystyle\leq Cw_{n-N}\sqrt{n-N}+c_{0}RNw_{n-N},

where
C>0
is a constant in Lemma
7.1
. Then letting
N=a_{n}
in
(A4)
integrating out
\mathcal{F}_{n-N}
, multiplying by
w_{n+1}\leq w_{n}
and summing over all
n\geq 1
gives

(179)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w_{n+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right|\right]\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}Cw_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}\sqrt{n-a_{n}}+c_{0}Ra_{n}w_{n}w_{n-a_{n}}<\infty.

Now, we establish
(i)
-
(v)
under case
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. This case must be dealt with separately since the stability of estimates
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
is not readily available (see Lemma
6.2
). In this case, we rely on ‘instant mixing’ (
\lambda=0
) of the i.i.d. data sequence
\mathbf{x}_{n}
stated in the second part of Proposition
8.1
. Namely, we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and use
\lambda=0
while avoiding using
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
. For
(i)
, we first use (
171
) with
a_{n}=0=N
to get

(180)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[w_{n+1}V(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]^{+}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}Cw_{n}^{2}<\infty.

The rest follows from (
172
). Then
(ii)
-
(iii)
can be deduced similarly as in the previous case using
(i)
. Also,
(iv)
follows from (
176
) using
\lambda=0
and
N=0
.
(v)
can be deduced similarly.

Lastly, we show
(vi)
. For cases
C1
-
C2
in Theorem
4.1
, this is trivial since we have
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
and
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}<\infty
by
(A4)
. Suppose
C3
. By Lemma
6.2

(i)
, we have

(181)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\geq\frac{\hat{\rho}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}-\Delta_{n}.

Moreover, using (
80
) and (
73
), we get

\displaystyle-\Delta_{n+1}\leq\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})+w_{n+1}(\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}))+w_{n}^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{k}.

Taking expectation and summing over
n\geq 1
and using
(i)
as well as
(A4)
,

(182)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})\right]\right|<\infty.

Using
(A5)
, this implies

(183)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\hat{\rho}}{2}\rVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{n}\right]<\infty,

as desired.
∎

Lastly in this section, we prove Lemma
7.3
.

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

Denote
\alpha_{n}=\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
and
\beta_{n}=\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})
. Fix
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\varepsilon>0
. With
(A1)
and a recursion,
\bar{f}_{n}
and
g_{n}
are also
R
-Lipschitz continuous. By Lemma
C.1
, we can write

(184)

\displaystyle\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}+\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2},

(185)

\displaystyle\left|\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}+\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\langle\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2},

for some uniform constant
L>0
that does not depend on
\boldsymbol{\theta},\varepsilon
and
n
. Recall that
\bar{g}_{n}\geq\bar{f}_{n}-\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. Hence

(186)

\displaystyle-\frac{L\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}+\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\langle\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}+\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta})

(187)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}+\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta})+\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}

(188)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n},\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle+\frac{L\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{n},

so, we obtain the following key inequality

(189)

\displaystyle\langle\alpha_{n},\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle=\textup{tr}\left(\left(\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right)^{T}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\theta})\right)\geq(\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{\varepsilon}_{n})-L\varepsilon^{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}.

Choosing
\boldsymbol{\theta}=-\alpha_{n}
, we get

(190)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}\geq(\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{\varepsilon}_{n})-c\varepsilon^{2}\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}.

Rearranging, we get

(191)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon-c\varepsilon^{2})\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}\leq(\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})).

Recall that this holds for all
\varepsilon>0
and
n\geq 1
. Taking expectation, multiplying by
w_{n}
, and summing up in
n
,

(192)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon-c\varepsilon^{2})\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})\right]+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}[\bar{\varepsilon}_{n}]<\infty,

where the finiteness above uses Lemma
7.2
and
(A4)
with (
72
). Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, this shows

(193)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty.

Next, we will analyze the sums
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{n}\right]\right\rVert^{2}
and
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\beta_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]
. To this end, note that

(194)

\displaystyle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\nabla\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\ell(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\nabla_{2}\ell(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right].

Define
\psi:(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\mapsto\nabla_{2}\ell(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})
. Then by the linearity of gradients and induction, we can show

(195)

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Thus by Lemma
7.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(196)

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right\rVert^{2}\leq Cw_{n},\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert^{2}\right]\leq Cw_{n}\sqrt{n}.

By
(A4)
, this implies

(197)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\,\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{n}\right]\right\rVert^{2}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\,\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]\right\rVert^{2}\leq C\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}<\infty.

Similarly, we also get

(198)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert\beta_{n}\right\rVert^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\,\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert^{2}\right]\leq C\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}.

Now we conclude the assertions. For
(i)
, by Jensen’s inequality, we have

(199)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\alpha_{n}\right]\rVert^{2}<\infty.

Hence by Fubini’s theorem, we get

(200)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\left(\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{n}\right]\right\rVert^{2}\right)\right]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\,\left\lVert\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{n}\right]\right\rVert^{2}<\infty.

This shows
(i)
.

Next, assume the optional condition in
(A4)
holds. since
w_{n}
is non-increasing in
n
, it follows that
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}<\infty
. Then (
198
) shows
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\beta_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty
. We have shown that
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty
in (
193
). Then noting that
\lVert\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}\rVert^{2}\leq 2\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}+2\lVert\beta_{n}\rVert^{2}
,
using Fubini’s theorem, we get

(201)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}\left(\lVert\alpha_{n}\rVert^{2}+\lVert\beta_{n}\rVert^{2}+\lVert\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}\rVert^{2}\right)\right]<\infty.

Since
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})=\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}
, this is the desired conclusion.
∎

9.
Proof of Lemma
7.5

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
7.5
. Throughout this section, let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an output of Algorithm
1
, where each update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
uses either Algorithm
2
(full coordinate descent with proximal regularization) or Algorithm
3
(block coordinate descent with diminishing radius). For the latter case, decompose the update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
into
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(0)}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(1)}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(m)}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
, where each
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}
is the output of (
13
) at sub-iteration
i
of Algorithm
3
. Namely, let
\mathbb{J}
denote the set of coordinate blocks used in Algorithm
1
and let
J_{1}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)\in\mathbb{J}
denote the coordinate blocks used in the
i
th iteration of Algorithm
3
for the update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}
is an approximate minimizer of
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)
over the convex set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J_{i}(n)}_{n}
(see (
12
)) with optimality gap
\Delta_{n}^{(i)}
(see
(A5)
), where
\Psi_{n}(x)
takes value
0
if
x\leq c^{\prime}w_{n}/m
and
\infty
otherwise (radius restriction). For a unified treatment, we also use the same notation when Algorithm
2
is used instead of Algorithm
3
. In this case, we take
m=1
(single update),
\mathbb{J}=\{\{1,\dots,p\}\}
,
J_{n}^{(1)}=\{1,\dots,p\}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n}^{J_{1}(n)}=\boldsymbol{\Theta}
(no frozen coordinates), and
\Psi_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{\hat{\rho}}{2}x^{2}
(proximal regularization). In this case, the optimality gap is denoted as
\Delta_{n}^{(1)}=\Delta_{n}
(see
(A5)
).

The starting point of the analysis in this section is the following proposition, which states that the linear variation of
\bar{g}_{n}
over the sequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n;i})_{n\geq 1,1\leq i\leq m}
is finite.

Proposition 9.1
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
. Under any of the three cases
C1
-
C3
in Theorem
4.1
, we have

(202)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle\right|\right]<\infty.

Furthermore, almost surely,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{J_{1}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)}\left[\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle\right|\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]<\infty.

Proof.

Fix
i=1,\dots,m
. By Proposition
6.1
,
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is
L
-Lipschitz for all
n\geq 1
. Hence by Lemma
C.1
, for all
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\left|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle\right|\leq\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\|_{F}^{2}.

Note that for
1\leq i\leq m
,

(203)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)+\Delta_{n}^{(i)}.

By induction, we get

(204)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})+\sum_{j=1}^{i}\Delta_{n}^{(j)}.

Hence

(205)

\displaystyle|\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})|\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})+2\sum_{J=1}^{i}\Delta_{n}^{(j)},

so it follows that

(206)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\|_{F}^{2}+\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})+2\sum_{j=1}^{i}\Delta_{n}^{(j)}

(207)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\|_{F}^{2}+\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+3\sum_{j=i}^{m}\Delta_{n}^{(j)}.

On the other hand, using (
80
) and
\bar{g}_{n}\geq\bar{f}_{n}
, note that

\displaystyle 0\leq\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})+w_{n+1}(\ell(\mathbf{x}_{n+1},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})).

Hence using Lemma
7.2
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1})\right]<\infty.

Next, by Lemma
7.2
, we have

(208)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}\|_{F}^{2}\right]<\infty.

Moreover, we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{n}\right]<\infty
by
(A5)
for cases
C2
and
C3
and by Lemma
C.6
for case
C1
. Thus the above inequalities yield

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle\right|\right]<\infty.

Taking
i=m
and using Fubini’s theorem, we obtain the first assertion (
202
). Furthermore, one can rewrite (
202
) as

(209)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{J_{1}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)}\left[\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle\right|\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]\right]<\infty.

Since the random variable inside the expectation above is nonnegative, it has to be finite almost surely. This shows the second part of the assertion.
∎

Next, we show that the block coordinate descent we use to obtain
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
should always give the optimal first-order descent up to a small additive error.

Lemma 9.2

(Approximate first-order optimality)
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
. Assume cases
C1
-
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. Then there exists constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
such that almost surely, for any sequence
(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
0<b_{n}\leq w_{n}
,

(210)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,b_{n}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})\right\rangle\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert}\right\rangle+\mathbb{E}\left[b_{n}^{-1}\Delta_{n}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

(211)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+c_{1}w_{n}+\mathbb{E}\left[c_{2}(1+\hat{\rho})b_{n}+c_{3}b_{n}^{-1}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right],

where we take
\hat{\rho}=0
for cases
C1
-
C2
. Furthermore, the above holds for all
b_{n}>0
for cases
C1
and
C3
.

Proof.

We first show the assertion for cases
C1
and
C3
. Fix arbitrary
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\Theta}
such that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}b_{n}
. Recall that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
is an inexact minimizer of
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Denoting
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}
to be an exact minimizer of the same problem and by using convexity of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, we have for each
a\in[0,1]
,

(212)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\Delta_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)-\Delta_{n}

(213)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})+\Psi_{n}(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)

(214)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}+a\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right)\right)+\Psi_{n}(a\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert).

Note that by Proposition
6.1
,
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
is
L
-Lipschitz and
\lVert\nabla g_{n+1}-\nabla g_{n}\rVert\leq L^{\prime}w_{n+1}
for some constant
L^{\prime}>0
. Hence subtracting
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})
from both side, we get

(215)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle-\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}-\Delta_{n}

(216)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle+\frac{a^{2}(L+\hat{\rho})}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}

(217)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle+\frac{a^{2}(L+\hat{\rho})}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+aL^{\prime}w_{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert,

where we have used
\Psi_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{\hat{\rho}}{2}x^{2}
for the first inequality. Rearranging, we get

(218)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle+c_{1}a^{2}(L+\hat{\rho})\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+aL^{\prime}w_{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert

(219)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+\Delta_{n}

for any constant
c_{1}\geq 1/2
. This holds for all
a\in[0,1]
. Viewing the right-hand side as a quadratic function in
a
, we can choose
c_{1}\geq 1
large enough so that it is non-decreasing in
a
. Indeed, the only possibly negative term is
a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle
, but its absolute value is at most
ac_{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert
for some constant
c_{2}>0
by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Proposition
6.1
. We will make a such choice for
c_{1}
. Then by choosing
a=b_{n}/\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert
, we obtain

(220)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq b_{n}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert}\right\rangle

(221)

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{1}b_{n}^{2}(L+\hat{\rho})+L^{\prime}w_{n}b_{n}+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+\Delta_{n}.

We have shown that the above holds for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
such that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}b_{n+1}
. It remains to argue that (
210
) also holds for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
with
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\geq c^{\prime}b_{n+1}
. Indeed, for such
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, let
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
be the point in the secant line between
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
such that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}b_{n+1}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
and (
210
) holds for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
replaced with
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
. However, the right-hand side is unchanged when replacing
\boldsymbol{\theta}
with any point on the line passing through
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
. Then the assertion follows by dividing both sides by
b_{n}
and taking the conditional expectation with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
.

Next, we show the assertion for case
C2
. The argument is similar but a bit more complicated due to the use of randomized block coordinate descent in Algorithm
3
. As before, fix arbitrary
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\Theta}
such that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}b_{n}
. We will suppress the dependency of random block coordinate
J_{i}(n)
on
n
below and denote it
J_{i}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n}^{J_{i}}
be as in (
12
), which is a convex subset of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
that contains both
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}+\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)})^{j}e_{j}
, where
e_{j}
denote the
j
th standard basis vector of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. It follows that

(222)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}+a\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)})^{j}e_{j}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J_{i}}_{n}\qquad\textup{for $a\in[0,1]$}.

Recalling that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}
is an approximate minimizer of
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n}^{J_{i}}
with gap
\Delta_{n}^{(i)}
(see
(A5)
),

(223)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)})-\Delta_{n}^{(i)}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i\star)})\leq\bar{g}_{n}\left(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}+a\left(\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)})^{j}e_{j}\right)\right),

where
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i\star)}
denotes an exact minimizer of
\bar{g}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n}^{J_{i}}
.
Then subtract
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)})
from both sides and use Lemma
C.1
. Note that by Proposition
6.1
,
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
is
L
-Lipschitz and
\lVert\nabla g_{n+1}-\nabla g_{n}\rVert\leq L^{\prime}w_{n+1}
for some constant
L^{\prime}>0
. Also, note that the coordinate blocks
J_{1},\dots,J_{m}
are disjoint so that each coordinate of
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
is updated at most once, see
(A6)
. This implies

(224)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)})^{j}e_{j}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j},\qquad\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert,\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\rVert\leq\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert.

So it follows that

(225)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\right\rangle-\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\rVert^{2}-\Delta_{n}^{(i)}

(226)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}),\,\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j}\right\rangle+\frac{a^{2}L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}

(227)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}),\,\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j}\right\rangle+\frac{a^{2}L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+aL^{\prime}w_{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert

(228)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j}\right\rangle+\frac{a^{2}L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+aL^{\prime}w_{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert

(229)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+aL\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert.

Also, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and
L
-Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
, we have

(230)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\right\rangle-L\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\rVert

(231)

\displaystyle\geq\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\right\rangle-L\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}.

Thus combining the above inequalities, we obtain

(232)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i-1)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j}\right\rangle+c_{1}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+a^{2}c_{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}

(233)

\displaystyle\qquad+ac_{3}w_{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert+ac_{4}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert+\Delta_{n}^{(i)},

where
c_{1},\dots,c_{4}>0
are constants.
Hence summing over
i=1,\dots,m
,

(234)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\right\rangle

(235)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq a\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j}\right\rangle+c_{1}^{\prime}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}+a^{2}c_{2}^{\prime}L\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}

(236)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+ac_{3}^{\prime}w_{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert+ac_{4}^{\prime}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert+\Delta_{n},

where we denoted
c_{i}^{\prime}=mc_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,4
. Note that (
234
) holds for all
a\in[0,1]
. By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Proposition
6.1
, and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is compact,

(237)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in J_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j}\right\rangle\right|,\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq c_{5}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert

for some constant
c_{5}>0
independent of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
(and hence
b_{n}
). Hence by choosing
c_{2}^{\prime}
in (
234
) large enough, we can make the right-hand side in (
234
) (viewed as a quadratic function in
a
) non-decreasing in
a
. We will make a such choice for
c_{2}^{\prime}
. Thus (
234
) holds for all
a\geq 0
. So we can choose
a=b_{n}/\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert
in (
234
), divide both sides by
b_{n}
, and take the conditional expectation with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
. Note that
(A6)
, for any
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
,

(238)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{J_{1}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j\in J_{i}(n)}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})^{j}e_{j}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]=\bar{c}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),

where
\bar{c}
denotes the common expected number of each coordinate
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
appearing in the coordinate blocks
J_{1}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)
. This will yield
210
where the right hand side is for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
such that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq c^{\prime}b_{n}
. By using a similar argument as before, it can be easily extended for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.
∎

Now we show Lemma
7.5
.

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

By using Lemma
9.2
with deterministic
b_{n}=w_{n}
and taking the conditional expectation with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, we have

(239)

\displaystyle w_{n+1}\left[-\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\right\rangle\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]

(240)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+cw_{n+1}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta_{n+1}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert^{2}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]

for some constant
c>0
for all
n\geq 1
. By Proposition
9.1
and
(A4)
, the right-hand side above except for the last term is summable. For the last term, note that by Lemma
7.2

(vi)
and Fubini’s theorem, we have

(241)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert^{2}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert^{2}\right]<\infty.

Hence
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert^{2}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\right]<\infty
almost surely. Thus the right-hand side of (
239
) is summable almost surely.
∎

10.
Proof of Lemma
7.4

In this section, we prove the only remaining key lemma, Lemma
7.4
, on asymptotic stationarity of the iterates with respect to the averaged surrogate loss functions. We proceed by first proving the assertion for cases
C1
and
C3
and then for the case
C2
in Theorem
4.1
.

Throughout this section, we will make use of the parameterized surrogate assumption
(A7)
. From this, there exists a compact set
\mathcal{K}
and a function
\hat{g}:\mathcal{K}\times\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that for all
n\geq 1
,
\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\hat{g}(\kappa_{n},\boldsymbol{\theta})
for some
\kappa_{n}\in\mathcal{K}
. Furthermore,
\hat{g}
is Lipschitz in the first coordinate. Hence by taking a subsequence of
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
, we may assume that
\kappa_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\kappa_{t_{k}}
. Hence the function
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{t_{k}}=\hat{g}(\kappa_{\infty},\cdot)
is well-defined. By continuity of
\nabla\bar{g}_{n}
(see Lemma
C.1
), we also have
\nabla g_{t_{k}}\rightarrow\nabla g_{\infty}
almost surely.

10.1.
Proof of Lemma
7.4
for cases
C1
and
C3
in Theroem
4.1
.

In this subsection, we prove Lemma
7.4
assuming cases
C1
and
C3
in Theroem
4.1
. The argument for case
C3
may require a bit of care since we cannot use per-iteration stability
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
(see Lemma
6.2
) and rely on the total stability
\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\right]<\infty
(see Lemma
7.2

(vi)
). Nontheless, Lemma
7.4
for both cases
C1
and
C3
can be shown using a simple argument that only uses
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=o(1)
.

We begin with a simple observation.

Proposition 10.1
.

Assume the cases
C1
or
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. Take
\hat{\rho}=0
for case
C1
and
\hat{\rho}>-\rho
for case
C2
. For each
n\geq 1
, let
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}
be the minimizer of the strongly convex function
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\bar{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\hat{\rho}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}
over the convex set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Then there exists a constant
\alpha>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(242)

\displaystyle\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}\rVert^{2}\leq\alpha\Delta_{n}.

Proof.

Note that
\bar{G}_{n}
is
\gamma
-strongly convex with modulus of convexity
\gamma=\rho>0
for case
C1
and
\gamma=(\hat{\rho}-|\rho|)
for case
C2
. Then the assertion follows from

(243)

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}\rVert^{2}\leq\bar{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{G}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\leq\Delta_{n}

for
n\geq 1
. Indeed, the first inequality follows from the second-order growth property (see Lemma
C.4
) since
\bar{G}_{n}^{(n)}
is a
\gamma
-weakly convex function minimized at
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, and the second inequality follows from the definition of optimality gap
\Delta_{n}
in
(A5)
.
∎

We are now ready to give proof of Lemma
7.4
for no regularization (case
C1
) and proximal regularization (case
C3
).

Proof of Lemma
7.4
for cases
C1
and
C3
in Theorem
4.1
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
,
(A7)
, and one of the cases
C1
or
C3
in Theorem
4.1
. Fix an arbitrary convergent subsequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}})_{k\geq 1}
of
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
. By taking a further subsequence, we may assume that the following limits
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{t_{k}}
and
\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{k}}
exist almost surely. It suffies to show that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

We keep the same notation for
\hat{\rho}
as in Proposition
10.1
. By using the first-order optimality of
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}\in\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left(\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\hat{\rho}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}\right)
, we have

(244)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})+\hat{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\right\rangle\geq 0\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}.

On the other hand, using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Proposition
10.1
,

(245)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+\hat{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\right\rangle-\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})+\hat{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\right\rangle\right|

(246)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})+\hat{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\right\rangle\right|

(247)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\rVert+\hat{\rho}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}\rVert\right)\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert

(248)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(L+\hat{\rho})\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert

(249)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(L+\hat{\rho})\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\,\sqrt{\alpha\Delta_{n}}.

Similarly, also note that

(250)

\displaystyle|\left\langle\hat{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\right\rangle|\leq\hat{\rho}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\leq\hat{\rho}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\,\sqrt{\alpha\Delta_{n}}.

Note that from
(A5)
,
\mathbb{E}[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{n}]=0
, and by Fubini’s theorem, this implies that
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{n}<\infty
almost surely. Hence
\Delta_{n}=o(1)
. Furthermore, since
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
converges, the sequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}})_{k\geq 1}
is bounded. So the above inequalities imply

(251)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim\,inf}\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\,\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\right\rangle\geq 0\qquad\forall\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}.

Since
\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{k}}\rightarrow\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, we conclude that
\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\right\rangle\geq 0
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, which means that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.
∎

10.2.
Proof of Lemma
7.4
for cases
C2
in Theroem
4.1
.

A key property of the iterates
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
for cases
C1
-
C2
in Theroem
4.1
is their stability,
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})
, which is given in Lemma
6.2
. For
C2
, which we consider in this subsection, we have an extra complication due to the use of an additional diminishing radius condition. Roughly speaking, for each limit point
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
of the iterates
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, we will need to show that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is not touching any trust region boundary we used along the way and it is indeed a stationary point of some limiting function of the averaged surrogates
\bar{g}_{n}
. The argument we provide here is adapted from
[
45
]
, which was first developed for (deterministic) block coordinate descent with diminishing radius.

We first show a sufficient condition for a subsequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}})_{k\geq 1}
to converge to a stationary point of a limiting averaged surrogate function.

Proposition 10.2
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
,
(A7)
, and the case
C2
in Theorem
4.1
. Further assume
\Delta_{n}=o(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert)
.
Suppose there exists a sequence
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
\mathbb{N}
such that almost surely, either

(252)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\rVert=\infty\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathop{\rm lim\,inf}\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\,\,\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{k}+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{t_{k}}\right]=0.

Then there exists a further subsequence
(s_{k})_{k\geq 1}
of
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{s_{k}}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}
exist almost surely and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
and is a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

Proof.

Proposition
9.1
, we have that for all
i=1,\dots,m
, almost surely,

(253)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\rVert\,\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{t_{k}+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\rVert}\right\rangle\right|\,\mathcal{F}_{t_{k}}\right]<\infty.

Thus the former condition in the assertion implies the latter almost surely. Thus it suffices to show that this latter condition implies the assertion. Assume this condition, and
let
(s_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a subsequence of
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
for which the liminf in (
252
) is achieved. Using
(A7)
, we may take a subsequence along which the following limits
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}
,
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{s_{k}}
, and
\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\nabla\bar{g}_{s_{k}}
exist.

Now suppose for contradiction that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is not a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Then there exists
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
and
\delta>0
such that

(254)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\right\rangle<-\delta<0.

Necessarily
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\neq\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
. By the triangle inequality, write

(255)

\displaystyle\lVert\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{s_{k}+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\right\rangle\rVert

(256)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{s_{k}+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}})-\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})\rVert\cdot\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}\rVert+\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})\rVert\cdot\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}\rVert.

By the choice of the subsequence
s_{k}
, we see that the right-hand side goes to zero as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Hence for all sufficiently large
k\geq 1
, we have

(257)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{s_{k}+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}\right\rangle<-\delta/2.

Note that by Lemma
6.2
,
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq cw_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
for some constant
c>0
. Applying Proposition
9.2
with
b_{n}=(c^{\prime}/c)\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert\leq w_{n}
, we get

(258)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert}\right\rangle\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\inf_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert}\right\rangle+c_{3}w_{n}

(259)

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\Delta_{n}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
for all
n\geq 1
. Hence by the hypothesis on
\Delta_{n}
, and that
w_{n}=o(1)
, denoting
\lVert\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rVert:=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert<\infty
, we conclude

(260)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim\,inf}\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\,\,\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{s_{k}+1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}),\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}\rVert}\right\rangle\right]\leq-\frac{c_{1}\delta}{2\lVert\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rVert}<0.

However, this contradicts the choice of the subsequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}})_{k\geq 1}
and the second condition in (
252
). This shows the assertion.
∎

Recall that during the update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{(1)}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{(m)}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
in Algorithm
3
, each block coordinate
changes by at most
c^{\prime}w_{n}
in Frobenius norm. For each
n\geq 1
, we say
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
is a
long point
if none of the block coordinates of
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
change by
c^{\prime}w_{n}
in Frobenius norm and
short point
otherwise. Observe that if
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
is a long point, then imposing the search radius restriction in (
13
) has no effect, and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
is obtained from
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}
by block-minimizing
\bar{g}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
without any radius restriction.

Proposition 10.3
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
,
(A7)
, and the case
C2
in Theorem
4.1
. If
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is such that
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{t_{k}}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
exist almost surely, then
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to that of
[
5
, Prop. 2.7.1]
. However, here we do not need to assume the uniqueness of solutions to minimization problems of
\bar{g}_{t}
in each block coordinate due to the added search radius restriction (recall that we assume
c^{\prime}<\infty
in the current section), and also note that we are considering a random choice of block coordinates in each application of Algorithm
3
.

Recall that
J_{1}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)
denotes the coordinate blocks chosen by Algorithm
3
at iteration
n
of Algorithm
1
, and by
(A6)
, their joint distribution does not depend on
n
and also they are independent of everything else. Fix a coordinate block
J\in\mathbb{J}
that has a positive probability of being chosen by
J_{1}(n)
. Then
J_{1}(t_{k})=J
for infinitely many
k
’s almost surely by a Borel-Cantelli lemma. We may refine the subsequence
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
so that
J_{1}(t_{k})=J
for all
k\geq 1
almost surely. For each
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, we write
\boldsymbol{\theta}=[\theta^{J},\theta^{J^{c}}]
, where
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{J}
is the projection of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
onto the coordinate block
J
and similarly for the complementary coordinate block
J^{c}
. In this fashion, we denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}=[\theta_{n}^{J},\,\theta_{n}^{J^{c}}]
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}=[\theta_{\infty}^{J},\,\theta_{\infty}^{J^{c}}]
. Recall the block update
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}-1}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}-1}^{(1)}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}-1}^{(m)}=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
and denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}-1;1}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}-1}^{(1)}
. Then since
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
is a long point,

(261)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{t_{k}}\left(\theta_{t_{k}-1;1}^{J},\,\theta_{t_{k}-1;1}^{J^{c}}\right)\leq\bar{g}_{t_{k}}\left(\theta,\,\theta_{t_{k}-1;1}^{J^{c}}\right)

for every
\theta\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J}=\textup{Proj}_{\mathbb{R}^{J}}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
. Indeed, in this case, his is because
\theta_{t_{k}-1;1}^{J}
is a minimizer of
\theta\mapsto\bar{g}_{t_{k}+1}(\theta,\theta^{c}_{t_{k};1})
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J}
. Noting that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert=O(w_{n})=o(1)
, it follows that

(262)

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{t_{k}}\left(\theta_{\infty}^{J},\,\theta_{\infty}^{J^{c}}\right)\leq\bar{g}_{t_{k}}\left(\theta,\,\theta_{\infty}^{J^{c}}\right)

for all
\theta\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J}
. Since
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{J}
is convex, it follows that

(263)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{J}\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}),\,\theta-\theta_{\infty}^{J}\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\quad\text{for all $\theta\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J}$}.

By using a similar argument for the subsequent coordinate blocks
J_{2}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)
, one can show that the above holds for every coordinate block
J
that has a positive chance of being chosen by some of the blocks among
J_{1}(n),\dots,J_{m}(n)
. The union of all such
J
covers the entire coordinates
\{1,\dots,p\}
by
(A6)
. Hence we deduce that
\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}),\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\rangle\geq 0
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. This shows the assertion.
∎

The following proposition gives a key property of a non-stationary limit point of the iterates
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, if exists.

Proposition 10.4
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
,
(A7)
, and the case
C2
in Theorem
4.1
. Suppose there exists a sequence
(n_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{n_{k}}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n_{k}}
exist almost surely and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is not a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}):=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}\,|\,\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\rVert<\varepsilon\}
with the following properties:

(a)

B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
does not contain any stationary points of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
;

(b)

There exists infinitely many
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}
’s outside of
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
.

Proof.

We will first show that there exists an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
of
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
that does not contain any long points of
\Lambda
. Suppose for contradiction that for each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a long point
\Lambda
in
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
. Then one can construct a sequence of long points converging to
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
. But then by Proposition
10.3
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is a stationary point, a contradiction.

Next, we show that there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
satisfies
(a)
. Suppose for contradiction that there exist no such
\varepsilon>0
. Then we have a sequence
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k})_{k\geq 1}
of stationary points of
\Lambda
that converges to
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
. Denote the limiting surrogate loss function associated with
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k}
by
\bar{g}_{\infty;k}
. Recall that each
\bar{g}_{\infty;k}
is parameterized by elements in a compact set according to
(A7)
. Hence by choosing a subsequence, we may assume that
\bar{g}_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\bar{g}_{\infty;k}
is well-defined. Fix
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
note that by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

(264)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq-\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})-\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty;k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k})\rVert\cdot\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}\rVert

(265)

\displaystyle\qquad-\lVert\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty;k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k})\rVert\cdot\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k}\rVert+\left\langle\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty;k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k}),\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k}\right\rangle.

Note that
\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty;k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k})^{T}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k})\geq 0
since
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty;k}
is a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty;k}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Hence by taking
k\rightarrow\infty
, this shows
\nabla\bar{g}_{\infty}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})^{T}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})\geq 0
. Since
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\textup{dict}}
was arbitrary, this shows that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is a stationary point of
\bar{g}_{\infty}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, a contradiction.

Lastly, from the earlier results, we can choose
\varepsilon>0
such that
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
has no long points of
\Lambda
and also satisfies
(b)
. We will show that
B_{\varepsilon/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
satisfies
(c)
. Then
B_{\varepsilon/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
satisfies
(a)
-
(b)
, as desired. Suppose for contradiction there are only finitely many
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}
’s outside of
B_{\varepsilon/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
. Then there exists an integer
M\geq 1
such that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}\in B_{\varepsilon/2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
for all
t\geq M
. Then each
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}
for
n\geq M
is a short point of
\Lambda
. By definition, it follows that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\lVert_{F}\geq w_{n}
for all
t\geq M
. This implies
\alpha_{n}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert^{2}}\geq\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert\geq w_{n+1}
for all
n\geq M
. Then by Proposition
10.2
, since
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}=\infty
, there exists a subsequence
(s_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}^{\prime}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}
exists and is stationary. But since
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}_{\infty}\in B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
, this contradicts
(a)
for
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
. This completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to give proof of Lemma
7.4
for the diminishing radius case.

Proof of Lemma
7.4
for cases
C1
-
C2
in Theorem
4.1
.

Assume
(A1)
-
(A4)
,
(A7)
, and the case
C2
in Theorem
4.1
. (As mentioned at the beginning of this subsection,
C1
is a special case of
C2
.) Suppose there is a non-stationary limit point
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
of
\Lambda
. By Proposition
10.4
, we may choose
\varepsilon>0
such that
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
satisfies the conditions
(a)
-
(b)
of Proposition
10.4
. Choose
M\geq 1
large enough so that
w_{t}<\varepsilon/4
whenever
t\geq M
. We call an integer interval
I:=[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]
a
crossing
if
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\ell}\in B_{\varepsilon/3}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\ell^{\prime}}\notin B_{2\varepsilon/3}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
, and no proper subset of
I
satisfies both of these conditions. By definition, two distinct crossings have empty intersections. Fix a crossing
I=[\ell,\ell^{\prime}]
, it follows that by triangle inequality,

(266)

\displaystyle\sum_{t=\ell}^{\ell^{\prime}-1}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}\rVert\geq\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\ell}\rVert\geq\varepsilon/3.

Note that since
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}
is a limit point of
\Lambda
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}
visits
B_{\varepsilon/3}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
infinitely often. Moreover, by condition
(a)
of Proposition
10.4
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t}
also exits
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
infinitely often. It follows that there are infinitely many crossings. Let
t_{k}
denote the
k^{\textup{th}}
smallest integer that appears in some crossing. Then
t_{k}\rightarrow\infty
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, and by (
266
),

(267)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}+1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\rVert\geq(\text{$\#$ of crossings})\,\frac{\varepsilon}{3}=\infty.

Then by Proposition
10.2
, there is a further subsequence
(s_{k})_{k\geq 1}
of
(t_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}^{\prime}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{k}}
exists and is stationary. However, since
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t_{k}}\in B_{2\varepsilon/3}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
, we have
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty}^{\prime}\in B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
. This contradicts the condition
(b)
of Proposition
10.4
for
B_{\varepsilon}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\infty})
that it cannot contain any stationary point of
\Lambda
. This shows the assertion.
∎

Appendix A
Background on Markov chains and MCMC

A.1.
Markov chains

Here we give a brief account of Markov chains on countable state space (see, e.g.,
[
43
]
). Fix a countable set
\Omega
. A function
P:\Omega^{2}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is called a
Markov transition matrix
if every row of
P
sums to 1. A sequence of
\Omega
-valued random variables
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is called a
Markov chain
with transition matrix
P
if for all
x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\Omega
,

(268)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{n}=x_{n}\,|\,X_{n-1}=x_{n-1},\dots,X_{0}=x_{0})=\mathbb{P}(X_{n}=x_{n}\,|\,X_{n-1}=x_{n-1})=P(x_{n-1},x_{n}).

We say a probability distribution
\pi
on
\Omega
a
stationary distribution
for the chain
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
if
\pi=\pi P
, that is,

(269)

\displaystyle\pi(x)=\sum_{y\in\Omega}\pi(y)P(y,x).

We say the chain
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is
irreducible
if for any two states
x,y\in\Omega
there exists an integer
t\geq 0
such that
P^{t}(x,y)>0
. For each state
x\in\Omega
, let
\mathcal{T}(x)=\{t\geq 1\,|\,P^{t}(x,x)>0\}
be the set of times when it is possible for the chain to return to starting state
x
. We define the
period
of
x
by the greatest common divisor of
\mathcal{T}(x)
. We say the chain
X_{t}
is
aperiodic
if all states have period 1. Furthermore, the chain is said to be
positive recurrent
if there exists a state
x\in\Omega

such that the expected return time of the chain to
x
started from
x
is finite. Then an irreducible and aperiodic Markov chain has a unique stationary distribution if and only if it is positive recurrent

[
43
, Thm 21.21]
.

Given two probability distributions
\mu
and
\nu
on
\Omega
, we define their
total variation distance
by

(270)

\displaystyle\lVert\mu-\nu\rVert_{TV}=\sup_{A\subseteq\Omega}|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|.

If a Markov chain
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with transition matrix
P
starts at
x_{0}\in\Omega
, then by (
268
), the distribution of
X_{t}
is given by
P^{t}(x_{0},\cdot)
. If the chain is irreducible and aperiodic with stationary distribution
\pi
, then the convergence theorem (see, e.g.,
[
43
, Thm 21.14]
) asserts that the distribution of
X_{t}
converges to
\pi
in total variation distance: As
t\rightarrow\infty
,

(271)

\displaystyle\sup_{x_{0}\in\Omega}\,\lVert P^{t}(x_{0},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}\rightarrow 0.

See
[
51
, Thm 13.3.3]
for a similar convergence result for the general state space chains. When
\Omega
is finite, then the above convergence is exponential in
t
(see., e.g.,

[
43
, Thm 4.9]
)). Namely, there exists constants
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
C>0
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

(272)

\displaystyle\max_{x_{0}\in\Omega}\,\lVert P^{t}(x_{0},\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}\leq C\lambda^{t}.

An important notion in MCMC sampling is “exponential mixing” of the Markov chain. For a simplified discussion, suppose in
(A2)
that our data points
\mathbf{x}_{t}
themselves form a Markov chain with unique stationary distribution
\pi
. Under the assumption of finite state space, irreducibility, and aperiodicity in
(A2)
the Markov chain
\mathbf{x}_{t}
“mixes” to the stationary distribution
\pi
at an exponential rate. Namely, for any
\varepsilon>0
, one can find a constant
\tau=\tau(\varepsilon)=O(\log\varepsilon^{-1})
, called the “mixing time” of
\mathbf{x}_{t}
, such that the conditional distribution of

\mathbf{x}_{t+\tau}
given
\mathbf{x}_{t}
is within total variation distance
\varepsilon
from
\pi
regardless of the distribution of
\mathbf{x}_{t}
(see (
270
) for the definition of total variation distance). This mixing property of Markov chains is crucial both for practical applications of MCMC sampling as well as our theoretical analysis. For instance, a common practice of using MCMC sampling to obtain approximate i.i.d. samples is to first obtain a long Markov chain trajectory
(\mathbf{x}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and then thinning it to the subsequence
(\mathbf{x}_{k\tau})_{k\geq 1}

[
6
, Sec. 1.11]
. Due to the choice of mixing time
\tau
, this forms an
\varepsilon
-approximate i.i.d. samples from
\pi
.

A.2.
Markov chain Monte Carlo Sampling

Suppose we have a finite sample space
\Omega
and probability distribution
\pi
on it. We would like to sample a random element
\omega\in\Omega
according to the distribution
\pi
.
Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)
is a sampling algorithm that leverages the properties of Markov chains we mentioned in Subsection
A.1
. Namely, suppose that we have found a Markov chain
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on state space
\Omega
that is irreducible, aperiodic
2
2

2

Aperiodicity can be easily obtained by making a given Markov chain lazy, that is, adding a small probability
\varepsilon
of staying at the current state. Note that this is the same as replacing the transition matrix
P
by
P_{\varepsilon}:=(1-\varepsilon)P+\varepsilon I
for some
\varepsilon>0
. This ‘lazyfication’ does not change stationary distributions, as
\pi P=\pi
implies
\pi P_{\varepsilon}=\pi
.
, and has
\pi
as its unique stationary distribution. Denote its transition matrix as
P
. Then by (
272
), for any
\varepsilon>0
, one can find a constant
\tau=\tau(\varepsilon)=O(\log\varepsilon^{-1})
such that the conditional distribution of

X_{t+\tau}
given
X_{t}
is within total variation distance
\varepsilon
from
\pi
regardless of the distribution of
X_{t}
. Recall such
\tau=\tau(\varepsilon)
is called the
mixing time
of the Markov chain
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
. Then if one samples a long Markov chain trajectory
(X_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, the subsequence
(X_{k\tau})_{k\geq 1}
gives approximate i.i.d. samples from
\pi
.

We can further compute how far the thinned sequence
(X_{k\tau})_{k\geq 1}
is away from being independent. Namely, observe that for any two nonempty subsets
A,B\subseteq\Omega
,

(273)
