\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A,\,X_{\tau}\in B)-\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A)\mathbb{P}(X_{\tau}\in B)\right|

(274)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A\,|\,X_{\tau}\in B)\right|\,\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{\tau}\in B)\right|

(275)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A\,|\,X_{\tau}\in B)\right|

(276)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A)-\pi(A)\right|+\left|\pi(A)-\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A\,|\,X_{\tau}\in B)\right|\leq\lambda^{k\tau}+\lambda^{(k-1)\tau}.

Hence the correlation between
X_{k\tau}
and
X_{\tau}
is
O(\lambda^{(k-1)\tau})
.

For the lower bound, let us assume that
X_{t}
is
reversible
with respect to
\pi
, that is,
\pi(x)P(x,y)=\pi(y)P(y,x)
for
x,y\in\Omega
(e.g., random walk on graphs). Then
\tau(\varepsilon)=\Theta(\log\varepsilon^{-1})
(see
[
43
, Thm. 12.5]
), which yields
\sup_{x\in\Omega}\lVert P^{t}(x,\cdot)-\pi\rVert_{TV}=\Theta(\lambda^{t})
. Also,
\mathbb{P}(X_{\tau}\in B)>\delta>0
for some
\delta>0
whenever
\tau
is large enough under the hypothesis. Hence

(277)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A,\,X_{\tau}\in B)-\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A)\mathbb{P}(X_{\tau}\in B)\right|

(278)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\delta^{-1}\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A\,|\,X_{\tau}\in B)\right|

(279)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\big|\left|\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A)-\pi(A)\right|-\left|\pi(A)-\mathbb{P}(X_{k\tau}\in A\,|\,X_{\tau}\in B)\right|\big|\geq c\lambda^{(k-1)\tau}

for some constant
c>0
. Hence the correlation between
X_{k\tau}
and
X_{\tau}
is
\Theta(\lambda^{(k-1)\tau})
. In particular, the correlation between two consecutive terms in
(X_{k\tau})_{k\geq 1}
is of
\Theta(\lambda^{\tau})=\Theta(\varepsilon)
. Thus, we can make the thinned sequence
(X_{k\tau})_{k\geq 1}
arbitrarily close to being i.i.d. for
\pi
, but if
X_{t}
is reversible with respect to
\pi
, the correlation within the thinned sequence is always nonzero.

In practice, one may not know how to estimate the mixing time
\tau=\tau(\varepsilon)
. In order to empirically assess that the Markov chain has mixed to the stationary distribution, multiple chains are run for diverse mode exploration, and their empirical distribution is compared to the stationary distribution (a.k.a. multi-start heuristic). See
[
6
]
for more details on MCMC sampling.

Appendix B
Example of Surrogate functions

In this section, we list some examples of block-convex surrogate functions, which include the usual convex surrogate functions in the literature (see,
[
46
,
47
]
).

Example B.1

(Proximal surrogates for
L
-smooth functions)
.

Suppose
f
is
L
-smooth, that is,
\nabla f
is
L
-Lipscthiz continuous. Then
f
is
L
-weakly convex, that is,
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}
is convex (see Lemma
C.2
). Hence for each
\rho\geq L
, the following function
g
belongs to
\surr_{L+\rho,\rho-L}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
:

(280)

\displaystyle g:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\rVert^{2}.

Indeed,
g\geq f
,
g(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})=f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
,
\nabla g(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})=\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
, and
\nabla g
is
(L+\rho)
-Lipschtiz. Moreover,
g
is convex being some of two convex functions:

(281)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\rVert^{2}=\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}\right)+\left(-L\langle\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\rVert^{2}\right).

Minimizing the above function over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
is equivalent to applying a proximal mapping of
f
, where the resulting estimate is denoted as
\textup{prox}_{f/\rho}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
(see
[
58
,
13
]
).
\blacktriangle

Example B.2

(Proximal modification of weakly convex surrogates)
.

One can also use proximal mapping to convert block weakly convex surrogates to multi-convex surrogates. Namely, suppose
g\in\surr_{L,-\rho}^{\mathbb{J}}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
is a surrogate of
f
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}
that is
\rho
-weakly convex in each block
J\in\mathbb{J}
. That is,
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert
is convex in
\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{J}
for each
J\in\mathbb{J}
. In this case,
g
is nonconvex in each block coordinate so it cannot be directly block-minimized. In this case, we can add a quadratic term to make it a multi-convex surrogate. That is, for each
\rho^{\prime}\geq\rho
, the following function
\tilde{g}
belongs to
\surr_{L+\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}-\rho}^{\mathbb{J}}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
:

(282)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto g(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\rVert^{2},

which can be easily verified by expanding out the quadratic term. In the slightly more general case when the modulus of weak convexity of
g
depends on the coordinate block
J
, we may add block-dependent quadratic terms to reduce the amount of proximal modification.
\blacktriangle

Example B.3

(Prox-linear surrogates)
.

If
f
is
L
-smooth, then the following quadratic function
g
belongs to
\surr_{2L,L}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
:

(283)

\displaystyle g:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})+\left\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\rVert^{2}.

(See Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C
.)
If in addition
f
is convex, then
g\in\surr_{L,L}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
; If
f
is
\mu
-strongly convex, then
g\in\surr_{L-\mu,L}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
.
\blacktriangle

Example B.4

(Prox-linear surrogates)
.

Suppose
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
where
f_{1}
is differentiable with
L
-Lipschitz gradient and
f_{2}
is convex over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Then the following function
g
belongs to
\surr_{2L,L}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
:

(284)

\displaystyle g:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})+\left\langle\nabla f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\right\rangle+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\rVert^{2}+f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}).

Minimizing
g
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
amounts to performing a proximal gradient step in
[
9
,
54
]
.
\blacktriangle

Example B.5

(DC programming surrogates)
.

Suppose
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
where
f_{1}
is convex and
f_{2}
is concave and differentiable with
L_{2}
-Lipschitz gradient over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. One can also write
f=f_{1}-(-f_{2})
which is the difference of convex (DC) functions
f_{1}
and
-f_{2}
. Then the following function
g
belongs to
\surr_{2L,L}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
:

(285)

\displaystyle g:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f_{1}(\boldsymbol{\theta})+f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})+\left\langle\nabla f_{2}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\right\rangle.

Such surrogates are important in DC programming (see, e.g.,
[
27
]
).

In fact, our method allows
f_{1}
to be only multi-convex with respect to the coordinate blocks in
\mathbb{J}
, that is,
f_{1}
is convex on
\textup{Proj}_{\mathbb{R}^{J}}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})
for each coordinate block
J\in\mathbb{J}
. In this case,
g
above is a multi-convex surrogate of
f
at
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}
and belongs to
\surr_{2L,L}^{\mathbb{J}}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
.
\blacktriangle

Example B.6

(Convex Variational Surrogates)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a two-block multi-convex function and let
\Theta_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\Theta_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two convex sets. Define a function
f_{*}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\inf_{H\in\Theta_{2}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
. Then for each
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\in\Theta
, the following function

(286)

\displaystyle g:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H^{\star}),\quad H^{\star}\in\argmin_{H\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{2}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star},H)

is convex over
\Theta_{1}
and satisfies
g\geq f
and
g(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})=f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
. Further, assume that

(i)

\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is differentiable for all
H\in\Theta_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz for all
H\in\Theta_{2}
;

(ii)

H\mapsto\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is
L
-Lipschitz for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\Theta_{1}
;

(iii)

H\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is
\rho
-strongly convex for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

Then
g
belongs to
\surr_{L^{\prime\prime},\rho}(f_{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
for some
L^{\prime\prime}>0
. When
f
is jointly convex, then
f_{*}
is also convex and we can choose
L^{\prime\prime}=L
.

\blacktriangle

Example B.7

(Multi-convex Variational Surrogates)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{m}}\times\mathbb{R}^{p_{m+1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a
(m+1)
-block multi-convex function and let
\Theta_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\Theta_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,m+1
be convex sets. Denote
\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=\Theta_{1}\times\dots\times\Theta_{m}
. Define a function
f_{*}:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\inf_{H\in\Theta_{p_{m+1}}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
. Then for each
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, the following function

(287)

\displaystyle g:\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H^{\star}),\quad H^{\star}\in\argmin_{H\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{p_{m+1}}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star},H)

is
m
-block multi-convex over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
and
satisfies
g\geq f
and
g(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})=f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
. Further, assume that

(i)

\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is differentiable for all
H\in\Theta_{p_{m+1}}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz for all
H\in\Theta_{p_{m+1}}
;

(ii)

H\mapsto\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is
L
-Lipschitz for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
;

(iii)

H\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta},H)
is
\mu
-strongly convex for all
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{m+1}}
.

Let
\mathbb{J}=\{J_{1},\dots,J_{m}\}
, where
J_{i}
denotes the
i
th coordinate block corresponding to
\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}}
. Then
g
belongs to
\surr_{L^{\prime\prime},\mu}^{\mathbb{J}}(f_{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
for some
L^{\prime\prime}>0
. When
f
is jointly convex, then
f_{*}
is also convex and we can choose
L^{\prime\prime}=L
.
\blacktriangle

Example B.8

(Refining block structure)
.

Suppose
f:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
convex, and we have a convex surrogate
g\in\surr_{L,\rho}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
. Let
\mathbb{J}
denote an arbitrary set of coordinate bloks for
\mathbb{R}^{p}
(e.g.,
\mathbb{J}=\{\{1\},\dots,\{p\}\}
). Then one can also view the fuction
g
as a multi-convex surrogate in
\surr_{L,\rho}^{\mathbb{J}}(f_{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
. Instead of minimizing
g
over the convex set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
with respect to all
p
coordinates, one can block-minimize
g
with respect to the block structure
\mathbb{J}
. For instance, if
\mathbb{J}=\{\{1\},\dots,\{p\}\}
and if
J_{i}(n)
in Algorithm
3
is chosen uniformly at random from
\mathbb{J}
, then Algorithm
3
becomes a random coordinate descent for
m
iterations, which choses a random coordinate
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and minimize
g
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
with respect to the
i
th coordinate. (See, e.g.,
[
67
]
).

A similar remark holds for multi-convex surrogates. Namely, suppose
g\in\surr_{L,\rho}^{\mathbb{J}}(f,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
, where
\mathbb{J}=\{J_{1},\dots,J_{m}\}
denotes a set of coordinate blocks for
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Let
\mathbb{J}^{\prime}
denote an arbitrary set of coordinate bloks for
\mathbb{R}^{p}
that refines
\mathbb{J}
. Namely, each coordinate block
J\in\mathbb{J}^{\prime}
is a subset of some
J_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
. Then one can also view the fuction
g
as a multi-convex surrogate in
\surr_{L^{\prime\prime},\rho}^{\mathbb{J}^{\prime}}(f_{*},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\star})
and accordingly, one can block-minimize
g
using the finer block structure given by
\mathbb{J}^{\prime}
instead of the original block structure
\mathbb{J}
.
\blacktriangle

See
[
46
]
for other types of surrogate functions such as quadratic, Jensen, and saddle point surrogates.

Appendix C
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma C.1

(Convex Surrogate for Functions with Lipschitz Gradient)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be differentiable and
\nabla f
be
L
-Lipschitz continuous. Then for each
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

(288)

\displaystyle\left|f(\theta^{\prime})-f(\theta)-\langle\nabla f(\theta),\,\theta^{\prime}-\theta\rangle\right|\leq\frac{L}{2}\lVert\theta-\theta^{\prime}\rVert^{2}.

Proof.

This is a classical Lemma. See
[
52
, Lem 1.2.3]
.
∎

Lemma C.2

(Characterization of weak convexity)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function. Fix a convex set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\rho>0
. The following conditions are equivalent.

(i)

(Weak convexity)
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}
is convex on
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
;

(ii)

(Hypermonotonicity)
\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle\geq-\rho\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
;

(iii)

(Quadratic lower bound)
f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})\geq\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle-\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}
for all
\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

Proof.

See
[
16
, Thm. 7]
for an equivalent statement for a more general case of local Lipschitz functions.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
: For
s\in[0,1]
, we have

(289)

\displaystyle s\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}\right)+(1-s)\left(f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})+\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}\right)\geq f(s\boldsymbol{\theta}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})+\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert s\boldsymbol{\theta}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}

so we get

(290)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})\geq\frac{f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}+s(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}))-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})}{s}-\frac{\rho}{2}\left(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}-\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}-\frac{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}+s(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})\rVert^{2}-\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}}{s}\right)

Taking the limit
s\rightarrow 0
, we get

(291)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})\geq\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle-\frac{\rho}{2}\left(\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}-\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}-2\langle\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle\right).

This implies
(iii)
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
: Adding the following two inequalities

(292)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle-\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}

(293)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})

\displaystyle\geq\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle-\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2},

we obtain
0\geq\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle-\rho\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}
. This implies
(ii)
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
: Fix
s\in[0,1]
and
\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Denote
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s}:=s\boldsymbol{\theta}+(1-s)\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
. Note that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}=s(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s}-\boldsymbol{\theta}=(1-s)(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\theta})
. By the mean value theorem, there exists
s_{*}\in[s,1]
and
s^{\prime}_{*}\in[0,s]
such that

(294)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta})=(1-s)\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\rangle,\qquad f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s})-f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})=s\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}^{\prime}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle.

Multiplying by
s
and
1-s
respectively and adding the resulting equations, we get

(295)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s})-sf(\boldsymbol{\theta})-(1-s)f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-s(1-s)\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{\prime}_{*}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rangle.

Note that since
s_{*}^{\prime}\leq s\leq s_{*}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}^{\prime}}
are in the secant line between
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}
, by
(ii)
,

(296)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{\prime}_{*}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert}{\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{*}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{\prime}_{*}}\rVert}\left\langle\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}})-\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{\prime}_{*}}),\,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s_{*}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{\prime}_{*}}\right\rangle

(297)

\displaystyle\geq-\rho\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{*}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s^{\prime}_{*}}\rVert\cdot\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert

(298)

\displaystyle\geq-\rho\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}.

It follows that

(299)

\displaystyle f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{s})-sf(\boldsymbol{\theta})-(1-s)f(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq s(1-s)\rho\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\prime}\rVert^{2},

which is equivalent to (
289
).

∎

Lemma C.3
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function such that
\nabla f
is
L
-Lipscthiz for some
L>0
. Then
f
is
L
-weakly convex, that is,
\boldsymbol{\theta}\mapsto f(\boldsymbol{\theta})+\frac{L}{2}\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}\rVert^{2}
is convex.

Proof.

Follows immediately by Lemmas
C.1
and
C.2
.
∎

Lemma C.4

(Second-Order Growth Property)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
be
\mu
-strongly convex and let
\Theta
is a convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Let
\theta^{*}
denote the minimizer of
f
over
\Theta
. Then for all
\theta\in\Theta
,

(300)

\displaystyle f(\theta)\geq f(\theta^{*})+\frac{\mu}{2}\lVert\theta-\theta^{*}\rVert^{2}.

Proof.

See
[
47
, Lem. A.4]
.
∎

Next, we provide some probabilistic lemmas.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of nonnegative random variables adapted to a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
\mathbb{E}[X_{n}]<\infty
for
n\geq 1
. Suppose there exists constants
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
M>0
such that for sufficiently large
n\geq 1
,

(301)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]\leq\alpha X_{n-1}+M.

Then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}X_{n}
exists and finite almost surely. In particular,
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is bounded almost surely.

Proof.

Fix a constant
c>0
and let
\widetilde{X}_{n}:=X_{n}+c
for
n\geq 1
. Substituting for
X_{n}
in (
301
), we get

(302)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tilde{X}_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}]\leq\alpha\tilde{X}_{n-1}+(1-\alpha)c+M

for large enough
n\geq 1
. Thus if we choose
c=-M/(1-\alpha)
, then
(\widetilde{X}_{n})_{n\geq N}
is a supermartingale with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\geq N}
for some
N\geq 1
. Furthermore,
\widetilde{X}_{n}
is uniformly lower bounded by
c=-M/(1-\alpha)
since
X_{n}\geq 0
for
n\geq 1
. By the martingale convergence theorem (see, e.g.,
[
18
, Thm. 4.2.11]
),
\tilde{X}_{n}
converges almost surely to some random variable, say,
\widetilde{X}_{\infty}
, such that
\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{X}_{\infty}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{X}_{0}]<\infty
. In particular,
\widetilde{X}_{\infty}<\infty
almost surely. It follows that
X_{n}
converges to
\widetilde{X}_{\infty}-c
almost surely. Lastly, note that

(303)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\textup{$(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}$ is not bounded })\leq\mathbb{P}(\textup{$X_{n}$ does not converge to $\widetilde{X}_{\infty}-c$})=0.

This shows the assertion.
∎

Lemma C.6
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
denote the output of Algorithm
1
with Algorithm
2
used for (
10
) with
\lambda_{n}\equiv 0
. Assume
(A1)
,
(A3)
, and
(A5’)
hold. Suppose that the data sequence
(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is contained in some compact subset
\mathfrak{X}_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
. Then
\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}\rVert=O(w_{n})
and
\Delta_{n}=O(w_{n}^{2})
for
n\geq 1
. In particular, if
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}<\infty
, then
(A5)
holds.

Proof.

The argument here follows the one given in
[
50
]
. Let
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}
be the exact minmizer of the
\rho
-strongly convex function
\bar{g}_{n}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
for
n\geq 1
. We introduce the following random variables

(304)

\displaystyle A_{n}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}\rVert,\quad B_{n}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}\rVert,\quad C_{n}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}\rVert,\quad D_{n}=\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star}).

By
\rho
-strong convexity of
\bar{g}_{n}
, almost surely,
\frac{\rho}{2}B_{n}^{2}\leq D_{n}
. By the second-order growth property (Lemma
C.4
) and using
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz continuity of
g_{n}
(see Lemma
6.1
), almost surely,

(305)

\displaystyle\frac{\rho}{2}C_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})

(306)

\displaystyle=(1-w_{n})\left(\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star})-\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\right)+w_{n}\left(g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star})-g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\right)

(307)

\displaystyle\leq w_{n}\left(g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star})-g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\right)\leq w_{n}L^{\prime}C_{n}.

This implise
C_{n}\leq c_{1}w_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
almost surely for some constant
c_{1}>0
. By triangle inequality, we can write
A_{n}\leq B_{n}+B_{n-1}+C_{n}
, so by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

(308)

\displaystyle A_{n}^{2}\leq(B_{n}+B_{n-1}+C_{n})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3(B_{n}^{2}+B_{n-1}^{2}+C_{n}^{2})

(309)

\displaystyle\leq 3((2/\rho)(D_{n}+D_{n-1})+(2L^{\prime}/\rho)^{2}w_{n}^{2}).

Next, we assume
(A5’)
and show that
D_{n}=O(w^{2})
. Denote
\widetilde{D}_{n}:=D_{n}/w_{n}^{2}
. We will show that

(310)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{D}_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\widetilde{D}_{n-1}+M

for all sufficiently large
n\geq 1
, where
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
M>0
are constants. Then by Lemma
C.5
, it follows that
\widetilde{D}_{n}
is uniformly bounded almost surely. This yields
D_{n}=O(w_{n}^{2})
. The assertion then follows by combining this with (
308
).

It suffices to verify (
310
). First, by using
(A5’)
and the previous inequalities involving
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
, we have

(311)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[D_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\mu)\left(\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\right)

(312)

\displaystyle=(1-\mu)w_{n}\left(g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})+g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n})-g_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\right)

(313)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+(1-\mu)(1-w_{n})\left(\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1})-\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star})\right)+(1-\mu)(1-w_{n})\left(\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star})-\bar{g}_{n-1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n}^{\star})\right)

(314)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\mu)L^{\prime}w_{n}(A_{n}+B_{n})+(1-\mu)D_{n-1}

(315)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\mu)c_{1}w_{n}\left(\sqrt{D_{n}+D_{n-1}+c_{2}w_{n}^{2}}+\sqrt{D}_{n}\right)+(1-\mu)D_{n-1},

where
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are constants. The second inequality above uses the fact that
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{n-1}^{\star}
is an exact minimizer of
\bar{g}_{n-1}
over
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
. Setting
\widetilde{D}_{n}:=D_{n}/w_{n}^{2}
, this gives

(316)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{D}_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-\mu)c_{1}\left(\sqrt{\widetilde{D}_{n}+\widetilde{D}_{n-1}(w_{n-1}/w_{n})^{2}+c_{2}}+\sqrt{\widetilde{D}_{n}}\right)+(1-\mu)\widetilde{D}_{n-1}(w_{n-1}/w_{n})^{2}.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Using the inequality
\sqrt{t}\leq\varepsilon t+\varepsilon^{-1}
for all
t\geq 0
, this gives

(317)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{D}_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\varepsilon\widetilde{D}_{n}+(1-\mu)(1+c_{1}\varepsilon)(w_{n-1}/w_{n})^{2}\widetilde{D}_{n-1}+c_{4}\varepsilon^{-1},

where
c_{3},c_{4}>0
are constants. Recall the hypothesis that
w_{n-1}/w_{n}\rightarrow 1
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Hence taking the expectation conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n-1}
and choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, we verify (
310
), as desired.
∎

Lemma C.7
.

Fix a bounded and measurable function
\psi:\mathfrak{X}\times\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Under Assumptions
(A2)
and
(A3)
,

(318)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\sqrt{n}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rvert\right]=O(1).

Furthermore,
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rvert\rightarrow 0
almost surely as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

Proof.

Omitted. See
[
42
, Lem. 10]

∎

Lemma C.8
.

Fix a continuous and measurable function
\psi:\mathfrak{X}\times\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Fix a sequence
(w_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
(0,1]
and define functions
\bar{\psi}_{n}:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

(319)

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{k}^{n}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta}),

where
w^{n}_{k}:=w_{k}\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}(1-w_{i})
. Suppose
(A2)
-
(A3)
hold. Assume that there exist an integer
T\geq 1
such that
w_{T}\geq 1/2
and
w_{n+1}^{-1}-w_{n}^{-1}\leq 1
for all
n\geq T
. Suppose
w_{n}\geq cn^{-\gamma}
for some constant
c>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
for all
n\geq 1
.

Then there exists a constant
C=C(T)>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(320)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rvert\right]\leq Cw_{n}\sqrt{n}.

Furthermore, if
w_{n}\sqrt{n}=O(1/(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\right]-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rvert\rightarrow 0
almost surely as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

The following lemma establishes the fluctuation bound in Lemma
7.1
for univariate observables.

Lemma C.9
.

Fix compact subsets
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
and a bounded Borel measurable function
\psi:\mathcal{X}\times\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Let
(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
denote a sequence of points in
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{n}=\varphi(X_{n})
for
n\geq 1
, where
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a Markov chain on a state space
\Omega
and
\varphi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
is a measurable function. Fix a sequence of weights
w_{n}\in(0,1]
,
n\geq 1
and define functions
\bar{\psi}(\cdot):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}\left[\psi(\mathbf{x},\cdot)\right]
and
\bar{\psi}_{n}:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
recursively as
\bar{\psi}_{0}\equiv\mathbf{0}
and

(321)

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{n}(\cdot)=(1-w_{n})\bar{\psi}_{n-1}(\cdot)+w_{n}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{n},\cdot).

Assume the following:

(a1)

The Markov chain
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
mixes exponentially fast to its unique stationary distribution and the stochastic process
(\mathbf{x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
on
\mathcal{X}
has a unique stationary distribution
\pi
.

(a2)

w_{n}
is non-increasing in
n
and
w_{n}^{-1}-w_{n-1}^{-1}\leq 1
for all sufficiently large
n\geq 1
.

Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(322)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert\right]\leq Cw_{n}\sqrt{n}.

Furthermore, if
w_{n}\sqrt{n}=O(1/(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then
\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left\lVert\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right\rVert\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
almost surely.

Proof.

We first argue for the special case when
w_{n}=1/n
for
n\geq 1
. In this case,
w_{n}^{-1}-w_{n-1}^{-1}\equiv 1
so
(a2)
is satisfied, and also
w^{n}_{k}\equiv 1/n
for
1\leq k\leq n
so
\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
becomes the sample mean

(323)

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{k},\boldsymbol{\theta}).

If
\mathbf{x}_{k}
’s are i.i.d. from
\pi
, then (
322
) holds by a standard empirical process theory. More generally under the hypothesis
(a1)
, (
322
) also holds by using a uniform functional central limit theorem for Markov chains. This statement has been shown in
[
42
, Lem. 10]
.

Now we consider a more general weighting scheme under
(a2)
. Fix
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
\Psi_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})=(t-i+1)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{j},\boldsymbol{\theta})
for each
1\leq i\leq t
. Denote
\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta}):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\pi}[\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})]
. By the previous case, there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
such that

(324)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}|\Psi_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})|\right]\leq\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{n-i+1}}

for all
1\leq i\leq n
. A simple calculation shows the following identity

(325)

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{n}-\bar{\psi}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(w_{i}^{n}-w^{n}_{i-1})(n-i+1)(\Psi_{i}-\bar{\psi}),

with the convention of
w^{n}_{0}=0
. Also, suppose
T\geq 1
is such that
w_{k}^{-1}-w_{k-1}^{-1}\leq 1
for
k\geq T
. Note that for
i\geq 2
,
w^{n}_{i-1}\leq w^{n}_{i}
if and only if
w_{i-1}(1-w_{i})\leq w_{i}
if and only if
w_{i}^{-1}-w_{i-1}^{-1}\leq 1
. Hence for each
n>T
and
k\geq T
, we have
w^{n}_{k}\leq w^{n}_{k+1}\leq\dots\leq w^{n}_{n}=w_{n}
. Then observe that

(326)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}|\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w^{n}_{i}-w^{n}_{i-1}|(t-i+1)\sup_{W\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left|\Psi_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\right]

(327)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w^{n}_{i}-w^{n}_{i-1}|(n-i+1)\,\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\left|\Psi_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right|\right]

(328)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w^{n}_{i}-w^{n}_{i-1}|c_{1}\sqrt{n-i+1}

(329)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\sqrt{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{T}|w_{i}^{n}-w_{i-1}^{n}|+\sum_{i=T}^{n}(\hat{w}^{n}_{i}-\hat{w}^{n}_{i-1})\right)

(330)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\sqrt{n}\left(w_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{T}w_{i}^{n}\right).

By using Lemma
C.10
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{T}w_{i}^{n}=O(1/n)
. Hence the above shows (
322
). Lastly, note that
(a2)
implies
w_{n}\geq c_{2}/n
for all
n\geq 1
for some constant
c_{2}>0
(see the proof of Lemma
C.10
) for details. Therefore we can conclude (
322
).

To show the second part of the assertion, suppose
w_{n}\sqrt{n}=O(1/(\log n)^{1+\varepsilon})
for some fixed
\varepsilon>0
. Denote
X_{n}:=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}}|\bar{\psi}(\boldsymbol{\theta})-\bar{\psi}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\theta})|
, which is a nonnegative random variable. We wish to show
X_{n}\rightarrow 0
almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Suppose for contradiction that there exists a diverging sequence
n(k)
such that
X_{n(k)}
does not converge to
0
with some positive probability. According to the first part, there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\mathbb{E}[X_{n}]\leq c(\log n)^{-1-\varepsilon}
for all
n\geq 1
. By Markov inequality, we have

(331)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|X_{n}|\geq\frac{1}{(\log n)^{\varepsilon/2}}\right)\leq\frac{c}{(\log n)^{1+(\varepsilon/2)}}.

We may choose a subsequence
m(r)=n(k(r))
of
n(k)
such that there exists a constant
c^{\prime}>0
for which
m(r)\geq c^{\prime}\exp(r)
for all
r\geq 1
. Then it follows that

(332)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|X_{m(r)}|\geq\frac{1}{(\log m(r))^{\varepsilon/2}}\right)\leq\frac{c}{r^{1+(\varepsilon/2)}}.

Since
m(r)
diverves, by the Borel-Cantelli lemma,
X_{m(r)}\rightarrow 0
almost surely as
r\rightarrow\infty
. This contradicts the assumption that
X_{n(r)}
does not converge to zero almost surely. This completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma was used in the proof of Lemma
C.9
.

Lemma C.10
.

Fix a sequence
(w_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of numbers in
(0,1]
. Denote
w^{n}_{k}:=w_{k}\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}(1-w_{i})
for
1\leq k\leq n
. Suppose
w_{n}^{-1}-w_{n-1}^{-1}\leq 1
for all sufficiently large
n\geq 1
. Fix
T\geq 1
. Then for all
n\geq T
,

(333)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{T}w^{n}_{i}=O(1/n).

Proof.

Suppose
w_{n}^{-1}-w_{n-1}^{-1}\leq 1
for all
n\geq N
for some
N\geq 1
. It follows that
w_{n}^{-1}-w_{N}^{-1}\leq n-N
, so
w_{n}\geq\frac{1}{n-N+w_{N}^{-1}}
. Hence for some constant
c>0
,
w_{n}\geq\frac{1}{n+c}
for all
n\geq N
. Denote
a\lor b=\max(a,b)
. Then note that

(334)

\displaystyle w^{n}_{k}=w_{k}\exp\left(\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}\log(1-w_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}w_{i}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\int_{N\lor(k+1)}^{n}\frac{1}{x+c}\,dx\right)=\frac{[N\lor(k+1)]+c}{n+c},

where the second inequality uses
w_{k}\leq 1
and the following inequality uses
\log(1-a)\leq-a
for
a<1
. Hence for each fixed
1\leq T\leq n
, we have

(335)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{T}w^{n}_{k}\leq T\left((N\lor(T+1))+c\right)\frac{1}{n+c}.

This shows the assertion.
∎

Remark C.11
.

In the original statement of
[
47
, Lem B.7]
, the assumption that
w_{n+1}^{-1}-w_{n}^{-1}\leq 1
for sufficiently large
n
was not used, and it seems that the argument in
[
47
]
needs this assumption. To give more detail, the argument begins with writing the empirical loss
f_{n}(\cdot)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}w^{n}_{k}\,\ell(\mathcal{X}_{k},\cdot)
, where
w^{n}_{k}:=w_{k}(1-w_{k-1})\cdots(1-w_{n})
, and proceeds with assuming the monotonicity
w^{n}_{1}\leq\dots\leq w^{n}_{n}
, which is equivalent to
w_{1}\geq 1-w_{1}
and
w_{k}\geq w_{k-1}(1-w_{k})
for
2\leq k\leq n
. In turn, this is equivalent to
w_{k}^{-1}-w_{k-1}^{-1}\leq 1
for
2\leq k\leq n
and
w_{1}\geq 1/2
. Note that this condition implies
w_{k}^{-1}\leq k+1
, or
w_{k}\geq\frac{1}{k+1}
. This means that, asymptotically,
w_{k}
cannot decay faster than the balanced weight
1/k
. We have used this observation in the proof of Lemma
C.10
.

Next, we will argue that
(A4’)
implies
(A4)
. This observation was made in
[
44
, Remark 20]
, but we repeat the argument here for completeness. It is clear that if the sequence
w_{n}\in(0,1]
satisfies
(A4’)
, then
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}=\infty
and
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}w_{n}^{2}\sqrt{n}<\infty
. So it remains to verify
w_{n}^{-1}-w_{n-1}^{-1}\leq 1
for sufficiently large
t
. Suppose
w_{n}=\Theta(n^{-\beta}(\log t)^{-\delta})
for some
\beta\in[0,1]
and
\delta\geq 0
. Let
c_{1},c_{2}>0
be constants such that
w_{n}t^{\beta}(\log t)^{\delta}\in[c_{1},c_{2}]
for all
n\geq 1
. Then by the mean value theorem,

(336)

\displaystyle w_{n+1}^{-1}-w_{n}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\left((n+1)^{\beta}(\log(n+1))^{\delta}-n^{\beta}(\log n)^{\delta}\right)

(337)

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\sup_{n\leq s\leq n+1}\left(\beta s^{\beta-1}(\log s)^{\delta}+\delta s^{\beta-1}(\log s)^{\delta-1}\right)

(338)

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\sup_{n\leq s\leq n+1}s^{\beta-1}(\log s)^{\delta-1}\left((\log s)+\delta\right).

Since
n\geq 1
, the last expression is of
o(1)
if
\beta<1
. Otherwise,
w_{n}=n^{-1}
for
t\geq 1
by
(A4’)
. Then
w_{n+1}^{-1}-w_{n}^{-1}\equiv 1
for all
t\geq 1
.
